Global Spherically Symmetric Solutions of the Multidimensional Full Compressible Navier-Stokes Equations with Large Data
Gui-Qiang G. Chen, Yucong Huang, Shengguo Zhu

Abstract
. We establish the global-in-time existence of solutions of the Cauchy problem for the full Navier-Stokes equations for compressible heat-conducting flow in multidimensions with initial data that are large, discontinuous, spherically symmetric, and away from the vacuum. The solutions obtained here are of global finite total relative-energy including the origin, while cavitation may occur as balls centred at the origin of symmetry for which the interfaces between the fluid and the vacuum must be upper semi-continuous in space-time in the Eulerian coordinates. On any region strictly away from the possible vacuum, the velocity and specific internal energy are Hölder continuous, and the density has a uniform upper bound. To achieve these, our main strategy is to regard the Cauchy problem as the limit of a series of carefully designed initial-boundary value problems that are formulated in finite annular regions. For such approximation problems, we can derive uniform a-priori estimates that are independent of both the inner and outer radii of the annuli considered in the spherically symmetric Lagrangian coordinates. The entropy inequality is recovered after taking the limit of the outer radius to infinity by using Mazur’s lemma and the convexity of the entropy function, which is required for the limit of the inner radius tending to zero. Then the global weak solutions of the original problem are attained via careful compactness arguments applied to the approximate solutions in the Eulerian coordinates.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q30, 35A01, 35D30, 76N10.

1.
Introduction

The motion of a compressible viscous, heat-conductive, and Newtonian polytropic fluid occupying a spatial domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 2

is governed by the following full compressible Navier-Stokes system (
CNS
) in the Eulerian coordinates:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+{\mathrm{div}}(\rho{\boldsymbol{U}})=0,\\
\partial_{t}(\rho{\boldsymbol{U}})+{\mathrm{div}}(\rho{\boldsymbol{U}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{U}})+\nabla P={\mathrm{div}}\,\mathbb{S},\\
\partial_{t}(\rho E)+{\mathrm{div}}(\rho E{\boldsymbol{U}}+P{\boldsymbol{U}})={\mathrm{div}}({\boldsymbol{U}}\mathbb{S})+\text{div}(\kappa\nabla e).\end{cases}

(1.1)

Here and throughout the paper,
\rho\geq 0
denotes the mass density,
{\boldsymbol{U}}=(U^{1},\dotsc,U^{n})^{\top}
the fluid velocity,

P
the pressure of the fluid,
E=e+\frac{1}{2}\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}\rvert^{2}
the specific total energy,

e
the specific internal energy,
{\boldsymbol{x}}=(x^{1},\dotsc,x^{n})^{\top}\in\Omega
the Eulerian spatial coordinates,
and
t\geq 0
the time coordinate. The equations of state for polytropic fluids are

P=R\rho\theta=(\gamma-1)\rho e,\hskip 19.91692pte=c_{v}\theta,\hskip 19.91692ptc_{v}=\frac{R}{\gamma-1},

(1.2)

where
R
is the gas constant,
\theta
the absolute temperature,
c_{v}
the specific heat at constant volume,
and
\gamma>1
the adiabatic exponent. The viscosity stress tensor
\mathbb{S}
is given by

\mathbb{S}=2\mu D({\boldsymbol{U}})+\lambda\,{\mathrm{div}}{\boldsymbol{U}}\,\mathbb{I}_{n},

(1.3)

where
D({\boldsymbol{U}})=\frac{\nabla{\boldsymbol{U}}+(\nabla{\boldsymbol{U}})^{\top}}{2}
is the deformation tensor,
\mathbb{I}_{n}
the
n\times n
identity matrix,

\mu
the shear viscosity coefficient, and
\lambda+\frac{2}{n}\mu
the bulk viscosity coefficient.
The constant,
\kappa
, in the energy equation is defined by
\kappa\vcentcolon=\frac{\kappa_{Q}}{c_{V}}
,
where
\kappa_{Q}\geq 0
is the coefficient of thermal conductivity. Furthermore,
(\mu,\lambda,\kappa)
satisfies the following physical condition:

\mu>0,\qquad\lambda+\dfrac{2}{n}\mu\geq 0,\qquad\kappa>0.

(1.4)

In this paper,
we are concerned with global spherically symmetric
solutions of the form:

(\rho,{\boldsymbol{U}},e)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)=(\rho(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert,t),u(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert,t)\dfrac{{\boldsymbol{x}}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert},e(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert,t))

of system (
1.1
)–(
1.4
) in domain
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with Cauchy data:

(\rho,{\boldsymbol{U}},e)({\boldsymbol{x}},0)=(\rho_{0},{\boldsymbol{U}}_{0},e_{0})({\boldsymbol{x}})=(\rho_{0}(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert),u_{0}(\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rvert)\dfrac{{\boldsymbol{x}}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert},e_{0}(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert))\qquad\,\text{for ${\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$.}

(1.5)

Therefore, for the spherically symmetric case with the radial coordinate variable
r=|{\boldsymbol{x}}|
, problem (
1.1
)–(
1.5
) can be reformulated as:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\partial_{r}(\rho u)+m\dfrac{\rho u}{r}=0,\\[2.0pt]
\rho\partial_{t}u+\rho u\partial_{r}u+\partial_{r}P(\rho,e)=(2\mu+\lambda)\partial_{r}\big(\partial_{r}u+m\dfrac{u}{r}\big),\\[2.0pt]
\rho\partial_{t}e+\rho u\partial_{r}e+P(\rho,e)\big(\partial_{r}u+m\dfrac{u}{r}\big)=2\mu\big(\lvert\partial_{r}u\rvert^{2}+m\dfrac{u^{2}}{r^{2}}\big)+\lambda\big(\partial_{r}u+m\dfrac{u}{r}\big)^{2}+\kappa\big(\partial_{r}^{2}e+m\dfrac{\partial_{r}e}{r}\big)\end{cases}

(1.6)

in
\Omega_{T}\vcentcolon=[0,\infty)\times[0,T]
with
m\equiv n-1
, and

\displaystyle u(0,t)=0,\qquad\partial_{r}e(0,t)=0

\displaystyle\text{for}\ \ t\in[0,T],

(1.7)

\displaystyle(\rho,u,e)(r,0)=(\rho_{0},u_{0},e_{0})(r)

\displaystyle\text{for}\ \ r\in[0,\infty).

(1.8)

The boundary conditions (
1.7
) is derived from the continuity of
{\boldsymbol{x}}\mapsto({\boldsymbol{U}},\nabla e)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
at
\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert=0
.

There is a large literature regarding the full
CNS
. For the general three-dimensional (3-D) flow, in the absence
of vacuum (
i.e.
,
\inf_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\rho_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})>0
), the local well-posedness of classical solutions of the Cauchy problem
for (
1.1
) follows from the standard symmetric hyperbolic-parabolic structure satisfying the well-known Kawashima’s
condition (
cf
.
[
22
,
21
,
28
,
36
,
37
,
38
]
), which was extended to the global one
by Matsumura-Nishida
[
34
]
for the initial data close to a non-vacuum equilibrium in some Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for
s>\frac{5}{2}
.
For both isentropic and non-isentropic flow,
we also refer the reader to
Danchin
[
7
,
8
]
for strong solutions
with small data in Besov spaces of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for
n\geq 2
,
and Hoff
[
16
,
17
]
for weak solutions with small and discontinuous data in some Sobolev spaces of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for
n=2
or
3
.
Considering the global well-posedness of classical or weak solutions with arbitrarily large initial data,
a subtle issue is whether, in the absence of smallness conditions, cavitation may occur in solutions of (
1.1
)
for barotropic and/or full
CNS
,
which has no clear answer yet so far, except the one-dimensional (1-D) case.
Specifically, for the 1-D flow,
the unique global classical solution was obtained by Kazhikhov-Shelukhin

[
30
]
for large initial data in a bounded interval with
\inf_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\rho_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})>0
, and
this theory was then extended to the unbounded domains in Kawashima-Nishida in
[
29
]
.
The key estimate developed in
[
30
,
29
]
is the pointwise upper and lower bounds of the density,
which have led to many notable results for the problems concerning 1-D flow (
e.g.
,
[
14
,
4
]
).
The global existence and stability of 1-D weak solutions with large and discontinuous initial data can be found in
Chen-Hoff-Trivisa
[
4
]
, Jiang-Zlotnik
[
27
]
, Zlotnik-Amosov
[
44
,
45
]
, and the references cited therein.
Furthermore, it was proved in Hoff-Smoller
[
19
]
that the weak solutions of the 1-D
CNS
do not develop
vacuum regions, as long as no
vacuum states are present initially.
While the multidimensional (M-D) case
is much more complicated: up to now, very few solid progress is known on the problems
of well-posedness or vacuum formation with large data.

It is worth pointing out that the approaches used in the references mentioned above do not work directly
when initial vacuum appears (
i.e.
,
\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}}{\rho_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})}=0
), which occurs when some physical requirements are imposed,
such as finite total initial mass and energy in the whole space.
One of the main issues in the presence of vacuum is the degeneracy of the time evolution operator,
which makes it difficult to understand the behavior of the velocity field near the vacuum.
In the terms of structures, system (
1.1
) is a hyperbolic-parabolic coupled system in the fluids region,
but degenerates to a hyperbolic-elliptic one near the vacuum region.
By imposing initially some compatibility conditions, Cho-Kim in
[
6
]
firstly established the local well-posedness
of 3-D strong solutions with vacuum in terms of
(\rho,u,\theta)
in some homogeneous Sobolev spaces,
which has been extended recently to be global ones with small energy but large oscillations by Huang-Li
[
20
]
, Wen-Zhu
[
40
]
,
and the references cited therein.

The first global existence of M-D weak solutions of finite energy for the isentropic
CNS

with generic data and vacuum was established in Lions
[
33
]
,
especially for
\gamma\geq\frac{9}{5}
when
n=3
;
see also Feireisl-Novotný-Petzeltová
[
9
]
for
\gamma>\frac{3}{2}
when
n=3
.
Moreover, this theory has been partially extended to the non-isentropic compressible flow with

(\mu,\,\lambda,\,\kappa)
depending on the temperature by Feireisl in

[
10
,
11
]
, where the thermal energy equation holds as an inequality in the sense of distributions.
However, the uniqueness problem of these weak solutions is widely open due to their fairly low regularity.
Also see

[
32
,
31
,
2
,
39
,
1
]
and the references cited therein.

For the M-D spherically symmetric flow, some favourable regularity properties may be expected, since the equations exhibit largely 1-D behaviour when the flow is away from both the origin and the far-field. However, compared with the 1-D case, in order to establish the existences of M-D spherically symmetric classical or weak solutions with arbitrarily large initial data of (
1.6
), some essential difficulties are encountered including the coordinate singularity at the origin, indicated by the singular factor
\frac{1}{r}
in system (
1.6
) and infinite initial total mass/energy from the far field;
also see
[
24
,
25
,
26
,
15
,
18
,
35
]
.

To circumvent these issues, it is customary to consider instead the initial-boundary value problem posed in some annular domains

\{({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,\infty)\,\vcentcolon\,a\leq\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\leq b\}

with proper boundary conditions, where
a>0
and
b>0
are both finite constants. The global well-posedenss of 3-D
strong solutions with cylindrical symmetry and strictly positive initial density to the full
CNS

has been given by Frid-Shelukhin
[
12
]
in an infinite cylinder of finite radius parallel to and centered
along the
x^{3}
–axis in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In the domain between a static solid core and a free boundary
connected to a surrounding vacuum state, Yashima-Benabidallah in

[
43
]
first established the global existence of spherically symmetric weak solutions with large and discontinuous initial data
to the full
CNS
with boundary data prescribed so that the uniform boundedness of the total energy of solutions is direct to achieve,
and later Chen-Kratka in
[
5
]
constructed the global large solutions in a more regular space with free normal stress on
the free boundary which is a natural physical situation for the fluids with the surrounding vacuum state.
Under the assumption that
0<\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}}\rho_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})<\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}}\rho_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})<\infty
, based on an elaborate truncation procedure on the initial data,
Jiang in

[
23
]
proved the global existence of spherically symmetric smooth solutions for (large) initial
data in the domain exterior to a ball

\{({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,\infty)\,\vcentcolon\,r=\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\geq a\}\qquad\,\,\,\mbox{in $\mathbb{R}^{n}$ for $n=2$ or $3$}

with the following boundary condition and the far-field behavior:

\begin{cases}u(a,t)=0,\ \ \partial_{r}e(a,t)=0\qquad\,\,\,\,\text{for $t\in[0,T]$},\\[2.0pt]
\lim\limits_{r\to\infty}(\rho,u,e,\partial_{r}e)(r,t)=(1,0,1,0).\end{cases}

A further natural question is whether the uniform estimates in
a>0
could be obtained so that
the region containing the origin can be included as
a\searrow 0
. In the absence of the vacuum,
Hoff
[
15
]
first constructed the global spherically symmetric weak solutions of the Cauchy problem
for the isothermal flow with large data and strictly positive initial density via the limit
process as
a\searrow 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 2
;
see also Haspot
[
13
]
for the case of isentropic flow with degenerate viscosity.
For the initial data with finite total initial mass and energy in the whole space,
Jiang-Zhang
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
proved the global existence of spherically symmetric weak solutions of the Cauchy problem
for the isentropic flow in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n=2,3
when
\gamma>1
.
Also see Xin-Yuan
[
41
]
for some analysis on the vacuum formation problems of these weak solutions.
Later, when
\inf_{\boldsymbol{x}}\rho_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})>0
,
Hoff-Jenssen in
[
18
]
proved the global existence of spherically symmetric weak solutions of the initial-boundary
value problems of the non-isentropic flow in a finite ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, which also works for the cylindrically symmetric case.
However, the carefully designed energy estimates and pointwise estimates applied in
[
18
]
depends strictly on the boundedness
of the domain under the consideration, which seems difficult to adapt the arguments to the corresponding Cauchy problem.
In fact, this Cauchy problem is highly nontrivial due to the strong coupling between the velocity and temperature,
the coordinate singularity at the origin, and infinite initial total mass and energy from the far field.
To the best of our knowledge, in the existing literature,
there have been no such results in this direction for the non-isentropic compressible flow with infinite energy.
The main aim of this paper is to establish the global-in-time existence of weak solutions of the Cauchy problem
for the full Navier-Stokes equations for M-D compressible heat-conducting flow
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 2
,
with initial data that are large, discontinuous, spherically symmetric, and away from the vacuum, and to analyze the underlying properties of these weak solutions.

To achieve these, our main strategy is to regard the Cauchy problem as the limit of a series of initial-boundary value problems (IBVPs) that are formulated in finite annular regions and, as the key argument, to establish a set of
a-priori
estimates that are uniform with respect to both the inner and outer radii of the annuli considered in the spherically symmetric Lagrangian coordinates. One of the first obstacles in deriving such estimates is the possible degeneracy or singularity of the temporal derivative operators
\rho\partial_{t}u
and
\rho\partial_{t}e
, which are influenced by the behavior of the density. This observation motivates the estimate of pointwise upper and lower bounds of the density; if it can be achieved, then the dissipation structure of the momentum and energy equations can be utilized to gain further information on the solutions.
Such
density bounds
are derived by
exploiting the underlying structure of
the continuity and momentum equations, and
the regional parameters in the derivation must be given careful consideration in order to obtain the density bounds
uniformly with respect to the size of domain.
For this, we face two major difficulties: The first is to ensure that such bounds are independent of
the inner radius
a\in(0,1)
of the annular region.
To deal with this, we use the crucial observation that the density bounds
can actually be expressed explicitly in terms of the coordinate variables
so that, using this explicit formula, the dependence on
a\in(0,1)
can be circumvented
by restricting to the spatial regions bounded away from the inner boundary.
The second difficulty is to show that the density bounds
are also uniform over the
outer radius of the annular region, which is resolved by taking the advantages of the specific form
of the entropy inequality for the solutions in the Lagrangian coordinates.
This is
the main motivation for our proof to start by constructing approximate solutions under the Lagrangian formulation of system
(
1.6
)
,
rather than their Eulerian form. Once the uniform pointwise bounds of the density are established,
it can be used to derive further estimates stemming from the parabolic structure of the momentum and energy equations.
However, suitable spatial cut-off functions have to be introduced in their derivations,
due to the fact that the density bounds described above are restricted on the domain bounded away from the inner boundary.
This leads to certain problematic integrals near the boundary that cannot be estimated with the standard parabolic argument;
we resolve this issue by incorporating the dissipation terms in the entropy inequality.
By overcoming all these difficulties, we obtain the desired domain-independent
a-priori
estimates,
which then allow us to construct a global weak solution of the Cauchy problem via the compactness argument.

We now outline the organization of the rest of this paper.
In §
2
, we first introduce the definition of weak solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.5
)
and then state our main results.
In §
3
, the main strategy and the overall scheme of the proof are described.
The Cauchy problem considered can be regarded as the limit of a series of initial-boundary value problems
that are formulated in finite annular regions.
For such approximation problems, in §
4
, we derive some
a-priori
estimates that are independent of
both the inner and outer radii of the annuli considered in the spherically symmetric Lagrangian coordinates.
In §
5
, we establish the existence of weak solutions of some exterior problems in the Eulerian coordinates.
Finally, in §
6
, the weak solutions of the original problem are attained via careful compactness arguments
applied to this set of approximate solutions in the Eulerian coordinates.

2.
Global Solutions and Main Theorem

In this section, we present the main theorem of this paper.
For this purpose, we first introduce the following definition
of global weak solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.5
).

Definition 2.1

(Weak Solutions)
.

The vector function
(\rho,{\boldsymbol{U}},e)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
is called to be a weak solution of
the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.5
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]
if it satisfies the following
:

(i)

There exists a upper semi-continuous function
\underline{r}(t)\vcentcolon[0,T]\to[0,\infty)
and a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(\rho_{0},{\boldsymbol{U}}_{0},e_{0},\gamma,\mu,\lambda,\kappa,n)>0
such that

\lim\limits_{t\searrow 0}\underline{r}(t)=0,\qquad 0\leq\underline{r}(t)\leq C_{0}\ \ \text{for all $\,t\in[0,T]$}.

With this, define

\mathcal{F}\vcentcolon=\{({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]\,\vcentcolon\,\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert>\underline{r}(t)\,\,\mbox{for $t\in[0,T]$}\}
as the fluid region.

(ii)

\rho\in L_{\textnormal{loc}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
,
\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},t)=0

for
({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T])\backslash\mathcal{F}
almost everywhere,

({\boldsymbol{U}},e)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
is locally Hölder continuous in
\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathring{\mathcal{F}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{F}\cap\{0<t<T\}
is an open set.

(iii)

For each
\Phi\in C^{1}\big([0,T];C_{\rm c}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big)
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\rho_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},0)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(\rho\partial_{t}\Phi+\rho{\boldsymbol{U}}\cdot\nabla\Phi)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s.

(iv)

\nabla{\boldsymbol{U}}\in L^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
.
Moreover, for any
\Psi\in C^{1}\big([0,T];C^{1}_{\rm c}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big)
with

{\mathrm{supp}}(\Psi)\Subset\mathcal{F}

(
i.e.,
{\mathrm{supp}}(\Phi)
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{F}
)
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(\rho U^{i})({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\Psi({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(\rho_{0}U_{0}^{i})({\boldsymbol{x}})\Psi({\boldsymbol{x}},0)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\big(\rho U^{i}\partial_{t}\Psi+\rho U^{i}({\boldsymbol{U}}\cdot\nabla)\Psi+(\gamma-1)\rho e\partial_{x^{i}}\Psi\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\big(\mu\nabla\Psi\cdot\nabla U^{i}+(\mu+\lambda)\partial_{x^{i}}\Psi\,{\mathrm{div}}\,{\boldsymbol{U}}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s\qquad\,\text{ for each $i=1,\dotsc,n$.}\end{aligned}

(v)

\nabla e\in L^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
.
Moreover, for any
\Phi\in C^{1}\big([0,T];C^{1}_{\rm c}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big)
with

{\mathrm{supp}}(\Phi)\Subset\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(\rho E)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(\rho_{0}E_{0})({\boldsymbol{x}})\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},0)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\big(\rho E\partial_{t}\Phi+(\rho E+P)({\boldsymbol{U}}\cdot\nabla)\Phi\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\Big(\kappa\nabla e+\dfrac{\mu}{2}\nabla\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}\rvert^{2}+\lambda{\boldsymbol{U}}{\mathrm{div}}{\boldsymbol{U}}+\mu(\nabla{\boldsymbol{U}})\cdot{\boldsymbol{U}}\Big)\cdot\nabla\Phi\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s.\end{aligned}

The main theorem provided below states the global-in-time existence and properties of weak solutions
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 2
.

Theorem 2.1

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
(\rho_{0},{\boldsymbol{U}}_{0},e_{0})({\boldsymbol{x}})=(\rho_{0}(r),u_{0}(r)\frac{{\boldsymbol{x}}}{r},e_{0}(r)),r\vcentcolon=|{\boldsymbol{x}}|
,
be spherically symmetric initial data defined in
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying

\begin{split}&e_{0}(r)\geq C_{*}^{-1},\quad C_{*}^{-1}\leq\rho_{0}(r)\leq C_{*}\qquad\,\text{ for all $r\in[0,\infty)$,}\\
&\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\rho_{0}\big(\dfrac{1}{2}\lvert u_{0}\rvert^{2}+\psi(e_{0})\big)+(\gamma-1)G(\rho_{0})+\lvert(\rho_{0}-1,u_{0}^{2},e_{0}-1)\rvert^{2}\Big)(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C_{*},\end{split}

(2.1)

for some given constant
C_{*}>0
, where
G(\zeta)=1-\zeta+\zeta\log\zeta
and
\psi(\zeta)=\zeta-1-\log\zeta
.
Then, for each
T>0
, there exists a global spherically symmetric weak solution
(\rho,{\boldsymbol{U}},e)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
of the
Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.5
) in the sense of
Definition
2.1

such that the following statements hold
:

(i)

There exists a continuous increasing function
g\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
g(y)\searrow 0

as
y\searrow 0
such that, for each bounded measurable set
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\qquad\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\rho\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}\rvert^{2}+G(\rho)\big)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq C(T),\quad\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{E}(\rho e)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq C(T)\big(1+g(|E|)\big),

where
|E|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
E
and
C(T)=C(T,C_{*},\gamma,\mu,\lambda,\kappa,n)>1
is a constant depending on
T>0
.
Moreover, for
\eta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\geq\underline{r}(t)+\eta}\dfrac{\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}\rvert}{\sqrt{e}}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C(T)\big(\eta^{\frac{2-n}{2}}+\eta^{2-n}\big)

\displaystyle\text{if }\ n=2,\,3,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\geq\underline{r}(t)+\eta}\log\big(\max\big\{1,e({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\big\}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C(T)\big(1+\sqrt{|\log\eta|}\big)

\displaystyle\text{if }\ n=2,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\geq\underline{r}(t)+\eta}\log\big(\max\big\{1,e^{\pm 1}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\big\}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C(T)\eta^{2-n}

\displaystyle\text{if }\ n=3.

(ii)

There exists a continuous function
(y,t)\mapsto\widetilde{r}(y,t)\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\times[0,T]\to[0,\infty)

such that
y\mapsto\widetilde{r}(y,t)
is strictly increasing, and
\underline{r}(t)=\lim_{y\searrow 0}\widetilde{r}(y,t)
for
t\in[0,T]
. Moreover,
there exists a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(C_{*},\gamma,\mu,\lambda,\kappa,n)>0
such that

\begin{cases}\int_{\{\underline{r}(t)<\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert<\widetilde{r}(y,t)\}}\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}=y&for a.e. $(y,t)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,T]$,\\
\widetilde{r}(y,t)=\widetilde{r}_{0}(y)+\int_{0}^{t}u(\widetilde{r}(y,s),s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s&for a.e. $(y,t)\in(0,\infty)\times[0,T]$,\\
ny\psi^{-1}_{-}(\dfrac{C_{0}}{y})\leq\widetilde{r}^{\,n}(y,t)\leq C_{0}(1+y)&for all $(y,t)\in(0,\infty)\times[0,T]$,\end{cases}

where
u(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert,t)\vcentcolon={\boldsymbol{U}}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\cdot\frac{{\boldsymbol{x}}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}
,
\psi^{-1}_{-}
is
the left inverse of
\psi(\zeta)=\zeta-1-\log\zeta
, and the function
y\mapsto\widetilde{r}_{0}(y)\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is implicitly defined as

y=\int_{0}^{\widetilde{r}_{0}(y)}\rho_{0}(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\qquad\text{for each $y\in[0,\infty)$.}

(iii)

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, there exists a constant
C(\varepsilon)=C(\varepsilon,T,C_{*},\gamma,\mu,\lambda,\kappa,n)>0
such that

\begin{cases}\lvert\widetilde{r}(y_{1},t)-\widetilde{r}(y_{2},t)\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert y_{1}-y_{2}\rvert&\hskip 10.22217ptfor all $(y_{1},y_{2},t)\in[\varepsilon,\infty)^{2}\times[0,T]$,\\
\left\lvert\widetilde{r}(y,t_{1})-\widetilde{r}(y,t_{2})\right\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert t_{1}^{\frac{3}{4}}-t_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}}\rvert&\hskip 10.22217ptfor all $(y,t_{1},t_{2})\in[\varepsilon,\infty)\times[0,T]^{2}$.\end{cases}

Moreover, let
\sigma(t)\vcentcolon=\min\{1,t\}

and
B^{\rm c}(r)\vcentcolon=\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\vcentcolon\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\geq r\}
. Then

C^{-1}(\varepsilon)\leq\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\leq C(\varepsilon),\,\,\,\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\sigma^{-\frac{1}{4}}(t),\,\,\,0<e({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\leq C(\varepsilon)\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t)

for a.e.
({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
such that
t\in[0,T]
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in B^{\rm c}(\widetilde{r}(\varepsilon,t))
, and

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{B^{c}(\widetilde{r}(\varepsilon,t))}\left\lvert(\rho-1,\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}\rvert^{2},e-1,\sqrt{\sigma}\nabla{\boldsymbol{U}},\sigma\nabla e)\right\rvert^{2}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle\,\,+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B^{c}(\widetilde{r}(\varepsilon,t))}\left\lvert(\nabla{\boldsymbol{U}},({\boldsymbol{U}}\cdot\nabla){\boldsymbol{U}},\nabla e,\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{t}{\boldsymbol{U}},\sigma\partial_{t}e)\right\rvert^{2}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C(\varepsilon).

In addition, for all
t\in(0,T]
and
{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{y}}\in B^{\rm c}(\widetilde{r}(\varepsilon,t))
,

\sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)-{\boldsymbol{U}}({\boldsymbol{y}},t)\rvert+\sigma(t)\lvert e({\boldsymbol{x}},t)-e({\boldsymbol{y}},t)\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{y}}\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}},

and, for all
0<t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T
and
\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\geq\sup_{t_{1}\leq t\leq t_{2}}\widetilde{r}(\varepsilon,t)
,

\sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(t_{1})\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}({\boldsymbol{x}},t_{1})-{\boldsymbol{U}}({\boldsymbol{x}},t_{2})\rvert+\sigma(t_{1})\lvert e({\boldsymbol{x}},t_{1})-e({\boldsymbol{x}},t_{2})\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert t_{2}-t_{1}\rvert^{\frac{1}{4}}.

Furthermore, defining

\rho^{(\varepsilon)}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\vcentcolon=\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\chi_{\varepsilon}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
with

\chi_{\varepsilon}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}1&if $t\in[0,T]$ and ${\boldsymbol{x}}\in B^{c}(\widetilde{r}(\varepsilon,t))$,\\
0&otherwise,\end{cases}

then, for all
L\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho^{(\varepsilon)}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in C^{0}\big([0,T];H^{-1}(B_{L})\big)
and

\big\|\rho^{(\varepsilon)}(\cdot,t_{1})-\rho^{(\varepsilon)}(\cdot,t_{2})\big\|_{H^{-1}(B_{L})}\leq C(\varepsilon)\left\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\right\rvert\qquad\text{ for all $0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq T$,}

where
C(\varepsilon)
is independent of
L\in\mathbb{N}
as before
and
B_{L}\vcentcolon=\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\vcentcolon\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert<L\}
.

Remark 2.1
.

The domain for which the momentum equations hold weakly can be extended to the entire space-time domain.
However, the viscosity term may be present in the weak form as a non-standard limit distribution
of approximate solutions.
More specifically, let
(\rho,{\boldsymbol{U}},e)({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
be a weak solution with initial data
(\rho_{0},{\boldsymbol{U}}_{0},e_{0})({\boldsymbol{x}})

obtained in
Theorem

2.1
.
Let
\xi\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
be a fixed increasing
C^{1}
–function with

\xi\equiv 0
on
[0,1]
and
\xi\equiv 1
on
[2,\infty)
,
and set
\xi^{R}(r)\vcentcolon=\xi(\frac{r}{R})
.
Then there exists a sequence of vector valued functions

\{{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\alpha}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\alpha}^{i}\in L^{2}_{\textnormal{loc}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T])

for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
and
i=1,\dotsc,n
.
Moreover, for each
i=1,\dotsc,n
and each
\Psi\in C^{2}\big([0,T];C^{2}_{\rm c}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big)

with
\Psi\vcentcolon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
, defining
the distribution
\mathbb{M}(\cdot,t)\in\big(C^{2}\big([0,T];C^{2}_{\rm c}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big)\big)^{\ast}
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{i}(\Psi,t):=\lim\limits_{R\searrow 0}\lim\limits_{\alpha\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\geq\alpha^{-1}\}}\big\{(\mu+\lambda)({\boldsymbol{U}}_{\alpha}\cdot\nabla)\partial_{i}\Psi^{R}+\mu\boldsymbol{U}_{\alpha}^{i}\Delta\Psi^{R}\big\}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s,

with
\Psi^{R}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\vcentcolon=\xi^{R}(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert)\Psi({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
,
then

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(\rho U^{i})({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\Psi({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(\rho_{0}U_{0}^{i})({\boldsymbol{x}})\Psi({\boldsymbol{x}},0)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\big(\rho U^{i}\partial_{t}\Psi+\rho U^{i}({\boldsymbol{U}}\cdot\nabla)\Psi+(\gamma-1)\rho e\partial_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{i}}\Psi\big)({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s+\mathbb{M}^{i}(\Psi,t)\quad\mbox{for each $i=1,\dotsc,n$}.

In fact,
{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\alpha}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)
is the velocity of solution to the exterior sphere problem, which satisfies the auxiliary boundary condition
:

{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\alpha}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)=0,\ \ \nabla e_{\alpha}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)=0\qquad\text{for $\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert=\alpha^{-1}$ and $t\in[0,T]$.}

The existence of such solution
(\rho_{\alpha},{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\alpha},e_{\alpha})
is stated
in
Theorem

3.1
in
§
3.3
.

The derivation of the above result is obtained by using the uniform entropy estimates on
the approximate solutions
(\rho_{\alpha},{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\alpha},e_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}

(
cf.
Theorem

3.1
(iii)(i)
;
also see
[
18
]
)
.
An important followup
is
whether the following equality hold
:

\mathbb{M}^{i}(\Psi,t)\overset{?}{=}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\big\{(\mu+\lambda){\mathrm{div}}{\boldsymbol{U}}\partial_{i}\Psi+\mu\nabla{\boldsymbol{U}}^{i}\nabla\Psi\big\}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s,

which is beyond the scope of this paper and will further be addressed in a separate
paper.

3.
Main Strategy and Reformulation

Throughout this paper, we use the following notation for five types of generic constants
that are all larger than
1
and may be different at different occurrence (without loss of generality)
to indicate their specific
dependence on various parameters:

\displaystyle C\equiv C(\gamma,\mu,\lambda,\kappa,n),\quad C_{0}\equiv C_{0}(C,C_{*}),\quad C(T)\equiv C(T,C_{0}),

\displaystyle C(a)\equiv C(a,T,C_{0}),\quad C(\varepsilon)\equiv C(\varepsilon,T,C_{0})\,\qquad\text{ for each $a\in(0,1)$ and $\varepsilon\in(0,1]$},

where
C_{*}=C_{*}(\rho_{0},u_{0},e_{0})>1
depends only on the initial data as
in (
2.1
) of Theorem
2.1
.
We also denote
\beta\vcentcolon=2\mu+\lambda
.

3.1.
Main strategy

Our approach for proving Theorem
2.1
is to split the original problem (
1.6
) into the following two parts:

(I)

For any fixed
a\in(0,1)
and
T>0
,
we prove the global-in-time existence of a weak solution
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})(r,t)

of the initial-boundary value problem (IBVP) of system (
1.6
)
in the exterior domain
(r,t)\in[a,\infty)\times[0,T]
with the following initial-boundary conditions:

\begin{cases}(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})(r,0)=(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)&\,\, for $r\in[a,\infty)$,\\
u(a,t)=\partial_{r}e(a,t)=0&\,\, for $t\in[0,T]$,\end{cases}

where
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})
is the approximate initial data constructed from
the initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0},e_{0})
given in Theorem
2.1
.
The detailed construction can be found in §
3.2
, and
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})
is shown to satisfy the
initial condition (
2.1
) with a constant
C_{0}>0
independent of
a\in(0,1)
in Propositions
3.1
.
Notice that
the corresponding solutions
\{(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})(r,t)\}_{a\in(0,1)}
satisfy certain high-order
estimates uniformly in
a\in(0,1)
(see Theorem
3.1
in §
3.3
).

(II)

We employ the uniform estimates, independent of
a
obtained in
(I)
,
and make careful compactness arguments to prove that there is a subsequence
a_{j}\searrow 0
as
j\to\infty
such that
the corresponding limit triple
(\rho,u,e)
is a global weak solution described
in Theorem
2.1
.

Part
(I)
of our approach will be achieved via the following six steps:

(I.1)

We modify the original initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0},e_{0})
into
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)
in the Eulerian coordinates, as constructed in §
3.2
.

(I.2)

The solution,
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})
, in
(I)
is obtained as the limit of solutions of the approximate problems
posed in bounded annular domains. For this purpose, we apply a truncation procedure on the mollified initial data
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})

in the Eulerian coordinates near the far field region (
i.e.
,
r\to\infty
), which is parameterized by integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(I.3)

We transform the IBVP in the exterior domain
(r,t)\in[a,\infty)\times[0,T]

stated in
(I)
into a set of IBVPs shown in (
3.15
)–(
3.16
) in the Lagrangian coordinates in the spatial interval
[0,k]
. Indeed, the main point of
the above reduction and modification of the initial data is to ensure that, for each
T>0
, there exists a unique global-in-time smooth solution of the approximate IBVP (
3.15
)–(
3.16
) in

(x,t)\in[0,k]\times[0,T]
.

(I.4)

We derive the entropy inequalities on these solutions. Consequently, we obtain the upper and lower bounds of the density.
These density bounds are independent of the approximation parameter
k\in\mathbb{N}
;
in addition, they are independent of
a\in(0,1)
on the regions away from the origin in the Lagrangian coordinates.

(I.5)

Based on the bounds of the density obtained in
(I.4)
, we seek for a set of high-order estimates
on the regions away from the origin,
x=0
, in the Lagrangian coordinates,
which is independent of the approximate parameter
a\in(0,1)
.
In fact, these estimates can be obtained by introducing a set of
C^{1}
cut-off functions
g_{\varepsilon}(x)
,
for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, away from the origin (see (
4.21
)–(
4.23
)).
However, the inclusion of these cut-off functions leads to the difficulties in estimating certain
integral terms near the boundary:
x=\varepsilon
, which is described in the proof of Lemma
4.4
.
In order to overcome these difficulties, we make full use of the dissipation terms in the entropy estimates from Step
(I.4)
, which can be found in Lemma
4.3
.

(I.6)

Finally, we denote
(\widetilde{v}_{a,k},\widetilde{u}_{a,k},\widetilde{e}_{a,k})(x,s)

(with the specific volume
\widetilde{v}_{a,k}:=(\widetilde{\rho}_{a,k})^{-1}
) as the approximate solution of problem (
3.15
)–(
3.16
)
in the Lagrangian domain
(x,s)\in[0,k]\times[0,T]
, and

\widetilde{r}_{a,k}(x,s)=\Big(a^{n}+n\int_{0}^{x}\widetilde{v}_{a,k}(y,s)\,{\mathrm{d}}y\Big)^{\frac{1}{n}}

as the corresponding particle path function.
For each
(a,k)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{N}
, we formulate the coordinate transformation
(r,t)=\mathcal{T}_{a,k}(x,s)=(\mathcal{T}_{a,k}^{(1)},\mathcal{T}_{a,k}^{(2)})(x,s)
as

\mathcal{T}_{a,k}^{(1)}(x,s)=\widetilde{r}_{a,k}(x,s),\quad\mathcal{T}_{a,k}^{(2)}(x,s)=s\,\qquad\text{ for $(x,s)\in[0,k]\times[0,T]$.}

Owing to the density bounds from Step
(I.4)
, the inverse function
\mathcal{T}_{a,k}^{-1}
exists.
By these coordinate transformations, we define

(\overline{v}_{a,k},\overline{u}_{a,k},\overline{e}_{a,k})(r,t)\vcentcolon=(\widetilde{v}_{a,k},\widetilde{u}_{a,k},\widetilde{e}_{a,k})(\mathcal{T}_{a,k}^{-1}(r,t))\ \qquad\text{for $(r,t)\in R_{a,k}$},

where
R_{a,k}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{T}_{a,k}\big([0,k]\times[0,T]\big)=\big\{(r,t)\,\vcentcolon\,t\in[0,T],r\in[a,\widetilde{r}_{a,k}(k,t)]\big\}
.
We then extend these functions into the entire exterior Eulerian domain
(r,t)\in[a,\infty)\times[0,T]
by introducing the following test functions:
Let
\xi\vcentcolon{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
be a function with
\xi\in C^{\infty}
such that
\xi(\zeta)=1
if
\zeta\leq 0
,
\xi(\zeta)=0

if
\zeta\geq 1
, and
\lvert\xi^{\prime}(\zeta)\rvert\leq 2
for all
\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
With this, we define the following cut-off functions:

\varphi_{a,k}(r,t)=\xi(\dfrac{2r-\widetilde{r}_{a,k}(k,t)}{\widetilde{r}_{a,k}(k,t)}).

It is shown in §
5.2
that
{\mathrm{supp}}(\varphi_{a,k})\subseteq R_{a,k}

so that the approximate solutions in the Eulerian coordinates can be extended
to domain
[a,\infty)\times[0,T]
:

\qquad\,\,(\rho_{a,k},u_{a,k},e_{a,k})(r,t)=(1,0,1)+\begin{cases}\varphi_{a,k}({\overline{v}}^{-1}_{a,k}-1,\overline{u}_{a,k},\overline{e}_{a,k}-1)(r,t)\quad\,\text{for $(r,t)\in R_{a,k}$,}\\
(0,0,0)\qquad\qquad\,\,\quad\text{for $(r,t)\in[a,\infty)\times[0,T]\backslash R_{a,k}$,}\end{cases}

(3.1)

With
the uniform
a-priori
estimates derived in Steps
(I.4)
–
(I.5)
,
there exits
a subsequence
(\rho_{a,k_{j}},u_{a,k_{j}},e_{a,k_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
its limit function
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})
is a weak solution of the exterior problem
stated in
(I)
. Furthermore, it can also be shown that such solutions continue to enjoy
the uniform regularity estimates in
a\in(0,1)
attained in Steps
(I.4)
–
(I.5)
.
The detailed analysis of this procedure can be found in §
5
.

Part
(II)
of our approach can be achieved via the following four steps:

(II.1)

For given approximate initial data
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})
in Part
(I)
, denote

\big\{(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})(r,t)\vcentcolon[a,\infty)\times[0,T]\to[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,\infty)\big\}_{a\in(0,1)}

as the global weak solutions of the exterior problem
obtained in Part
(I)
. Based on the entropy estimate, we prove the uniform integrability of the approximate
solutions
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})_{a\in(0,1)}
with respect to the approximate parameter
a\in(0,1)
in §
6.1
,
which plays key roles in proving that the limit vector function of these approximate solutions
are indeed a weak solution of the original problem (see Lemmas
6.8
–
6.9
).

(II.2)

The particle path functions
\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t)\vcentcolon[0,\infty]\times[0,T]\to[a,\infty)

associated with the approximate density
\rho_{a}
for each
a\in(0,1)
are defined to be the functions satisfying

x=\int_{a}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t)}\rho_{a}(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\qquad\,\,\text{for {\it a.e.} $(x,t)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,T]$,}

which can be found in Lemma
5.3
in §
5.3
.
Then, by the estimates obtained in Step
(II.1)
and compactness arguments,
we show in §
6.2
that there exists a function
\widetilde{r}(x,t)\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\times[0,T]\to[0,\infty)
that
is the limit function of sequence
\{\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t)\}_{a\in(0,1)}
when
a\searrow 0
.
Moreover, we show that, for
t\in[0,T]
,
\lim_{x\searrow 0}\widetilde{r}(x,t)
does not necessarily equal to zero,
but a function of
t
instead in general:
\underline{r}(t)\vcentcolon[0,T]\to[0,\infty)
.
In fact,
\widetilde{r}(x,t)
is the particle path of our original problem (
1.6
),
and
\underline{r}(t)
is the vacuum interface in (
1.6
) that separates the fluid region
and the possible vacuum, which can be verified later.
Furthermore, it is shown in Lemma
6.2
(iii)(iii)
that,
for each
x>0
, there exists
\delta(x)>0
depending only on
x>0

such that

\inf_{t\in[0,T]}\widetilde{r}(x,t)\geq\delta(x)>0.

(3.2)

This
is crucial for the next step where the limit,
a\searrow 0
, is taken in
\{(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})\}_{a\in(0,1)}
.

(II.3)

Next, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we fix a domain away from the origin by

\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\big\{(r,t)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,T]\,\vcentcolon\,r\in[\widetilde{r}(\varepsilon,t),\infty)\big\},

(3.3)

where
\widetilde{r}(x,t)
is obtained in Step
(II.2)
.
Moreover, we set

\mathcal{F}\vcentcolon=\{(r,t)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,T]\,\vcentcolon\,r>\underline{r}(t)\}.

By the high-order estimates derived in Step
(I.5)
, a limit function
(\rho,u,e)(r,t)\vcentcolon\mathcal{F}\to[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,\infty)
is constructed in §
6.3

as follows: By an application of the Arzelá-Ascoli theorem, we first obtain
(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon},e_{\varepsilon})(r,t)
defined in domain
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
then construct the vector function
(\rho,u,e)
by extending
(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon},e_{\varepsilon})
with

(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon},e_{\varepsilon})(r,t)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&if $(r,t)\in\mathcal{F}\backslash\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}$,\\
(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon},e_{\varepsilon})(r,t)&if $(r,t)\in\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}$,\end{cases}

and finally glue
\big\{(\rho_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon},e_{\varepsilon})(r,t)\big\}_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}
together
over parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
by the telescoping sum:

f=f_{\varepsilon_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(f_{\varepsilon_{i+1}}-f_{\varepsilon_{i}}),

for
f=\rho,u,e
, respectively, and
\varepsilon_{i}\equiv\frac{1}{i}
.
The detail of this procedure is given in Propositions
A.1
–
A.2
in Appendix
A
.
In addition, it is also shown in Lemma
6.4
that

x=\int_{\underline{r}(t)}^{\widetilde{r}(x,t)}\rho(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\qquad\text{for {\it a.e.} $(x,t)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,T]$,}

which justifies that
\underline{r}(t)
obtained in Step
(II.2)
is the vacuum interface curve of our original problem (
1.6
).

(II.4)

Finally, by the estimates obtained in Step
(II.1)
and the convergences in Steps
(II.2)
–
(II.3)
,
we prove the weak forms of solutions listed in Theorem
2.1
.
The weak forms of solutions in the Eulerian coordinates
({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]
are shown in §
6.4
.

Remark 3.1
.

Before proceeding, we first give the following remarks
:

(a)

In Steps
(I.1)
–
(I.2)
, it is important to show that the
modified initial data sequence satisfies the entropy estimates uniformly with respect
to
(a,k)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{N}
and strongly
converges to the original initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0},e_{0})(r)

in proper functional spaces. These results will be needed later in the compactness
arguments in Step
(I.6)
, which is detailed in
§
5
.

(b)

The main purpose of the reformulation from the Eulerian to the Lagrangian coordinates
in Step
(I.3)
is to obtain the crucial upper and lower bounds of
the density
(
see statement
(iii)(ii)
of
Theorem
4.1

in
§
4
)
, which are independent of
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Note that these bounds of the density are an improvement on the result of Jiang
[
23
]
.

(c)

As mentioned in Step
(I.5)
, the introduction of

C^{1}
cut-off functions away from the origin leads to several hurdles in the estimates,
and these issues have also been encountered in Hoff-Jenssen
[
18
]
.
In this paper, we resolve these issues by making use of the dissipation terms that are present
in the entropy estimate. This is detailed in
Lemma
4.3

in
§
4.3
.

(d)

The purpose of the coordinate transformation back into the Eulerian domain,
introduced in Step
(I.6)
is due to the following consideration
:

As
k\to\infty
, a difficulty arises due to the nonlinear second-order spatial
differential operators in the Lagrangian forms of the momentum and internal energy equations.
Since these operators are linear in the Eulerian coordinates, this difficulty is resolved by
converting the approximate solutions, originally obtained in the Lagrangian coordinates,
into the functions
in the Eulerian domain. This allows us to take limit
k\to\infty
and
obtain the desired weak forms in the Eulerian coordinates.

(e)

In Step
(I.6)
, after taking limit
k\to\infty
,
it requires to show that the limit solution still satisfies the entropy inequality,
since it will be required in Step
(II.1)
.
This is achieved by an application of Mazur’s lemma
(
Lemma
5.9
)

in
§
5.5
and
Proposition
B.2
.

(f)

In order to apply the compactness arguments from the high-order estimates obtained in
(I.5)
,
it is crucial that
\mathcal{F}_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
3.3
) is a set strictly bounded away from the
origin,
r=0
, for each
\varepsilon>0
.
We obtain this by assertion (
3.2
) in Step
(II.2)
.

The rest of this section is organized as follows: In §
3.3
, we show our main existence
results for
weak solutions with large data of the exterior problem mentioned in Part
(I)
. In §
3.2
– §
3.4
,
we describe the detailed procedures for the modification and truncation of the initial data listed in Steps
(I.2)
–
(I.3)
above.
Finally, in §
3.5
, we reformulate the exterior problem in the Eulerian coordinates into the approximate Lagrangian problems
in domain
(x,t)\in[0,k]\times[0,T]
as described in Step
(I.4)
,
and establish the corresponding global-in-time well-posedness of the unique strong solution with regular initial data.

3.2.
Mollification of the initial data in the Eulerian domain

Let
(\rho_{0},u_{0},e_{0})(r)
,
r\in[0,\infty)
, be the initial data in Theorem
2.1
.
First, we extend the initial data to

\mathbb{R}

by

(\hat{\rho}_{0},\hat{u}_{0},\hat{e}_{0})(\zeta)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}(\rho_{0}(\zeta),u_{0}(\zeta),e_{0}(\zeta))&if $\zeta\in[0,\infty)$,\\[2.84526pt]
(\rho_{0}(-\zeta),-u_{0}(-\zeta),e_{0}(-\zeta))&if $\zeta\in(-\infty,0)$.\end{cases}

(3.4)

Then, for each
a\in(0,1)
, let
j_{a}\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}([-1,1])
be
the standard 1-D mollifiers such that
{\mathrm{supp}}(j_{a})\subseteq[-\frac{a}{2},\frac{a}{2}]
. We define

(\hat{\rho}_{a}^{0},\hat{u}_{a}^{0},\hat{e}_{a}^{0})\vcentcolon=(\hat{\rho}_{0}\ast j_{a},\hat{u}_{0}\ast j_{a},\hat{e}_{0}\ast j_{a}).

It follows that, for some
C_{*}>0
, independent of
a\in(0,1)
,

\begin{split}&(\hat{\rho}_{a}^{0},\hat{u}_{a}^{0},\hat{e}_{a}^{0})\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}),\\
&\hat{e}_{a}^{0}(\zeta)\geq C_{*}^{-1},\,\,\,\,C_{*}^{-1}\leq\hat{\rho}_{a}^{0}(\zeta)\leq C_{*}\qquad\ \text{for {\it a.e.} $\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}$,}\\
&(\hat{\rho}_{a}^{0},\hat{u}_{a}^{0},\hat{e}_{a}^{0})(\zeta)\to(\rho_{0},u_{0},e_{0})(r)\qquad\quad\ \text{as $a\searrow 0$ for {\it a.e.} $\zeta\in(0,\infty)$.}\end{split}

(3.5)

Next, let
\chi\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
be such that

\chi\in C^{\infty}
,
\chi(\zeta)=0
if
\zeta\leq 1
, and
\chi(\zeta)=1
if
\zeta\geq 2
.
Set the cut-off functions:

\chi_{a}(r)=\chi(\frac{r}{a})\qquad\mbox{for each $a\in(0,1)$}.

It follows that
\chi_{a}(r)=0
for
r\leq a
and
\chi_{a}(r)=1
for
r\geq 2a
.
With this, we define the approximate exterior initial data:

(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)\vcentcolon=\big((\hat{\rho}_{a}^{0}-1)\chi_{a}+1,\,\hat{u}_{a}^{0}\chi_{a},\,(\hat{e}_{a}^{0}-1)\chi_{a}+1\big)(r)\qquad\text{for all $r\in(0,\infty)$.}

(3.6)

Proposition 3.1
.

The approximate exterior initial data
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)
satisfy

item (i)(i)

(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty))

and
u_{a}^{0}(a)=\partial_{r}e_{a}^{0}(a)=0
.

item (ii)(ii)

As
a\searrow 0
,

\begin{split}&\lVert(\rho_{a}^{0}-1,\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{2},e_{a}^{0}-1)-(\rho_{0}-1,\lvert u_{0}\rvert^{2},e_{0}-1)\rVert_{L^{2}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\to 0,\\
&(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)\to(\rho_{0},u_{0},e_{0})(r)\qquad\ \text{for a.e. $r\in[0,\infty)$.}\end{split}

(3.7)

item (iii)(iii)

There exists some constant
C_{0}>0
independent of
a\in(0,1)
such that

\begin{split}&\,\,e_{a}^{0}(r)\geq C_{0}^{-1},\ \ \,C_{0}^{-1}\leq\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\leq C_{0}\qquad\ \text{ for all $(a,r)\in(0,1)\times[0,\infty)$,}\\
&\sup\limits_{a\in(0,1)}\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big(\rho_{a}^{0}\big(\frac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{2}+\psi(e_{a}^{0})\big)+G(\rho_{a}^{0})+\lvert(\rho_{a}^{0}-1,|u_{a}^{0}|^{2},e_{a}^{0}-1)\rvert^{2}\Big)(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C_{0}.\end{split}

(3.8)

Proof.

Statements
(i)
and

C_{*}^{-1}\leq\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\leq C_{*}

for all
r\in[0,\infty)
follow immediately from construction (
3.6
),
while the almost everywhere convergence
\eqref{temp:mollify1}_{2}
follows from (
3.5
).

Next, by the Minkowski
inequality and (
3.6
), we have

\displaystyle\lVert u_{a}^{0}\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lvert\chi_{a}(r)\hat{u}_{0}(r-\zeta)j_{a}(\zeta)\rvert^{4}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\leq\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{a}^{\infty}\lvert u_{0}(r-\zeta)\rvert^{4}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta.

Let
\xi\equiv r-\zeta
.
Then
0\leq 1+\frac{\zeta}{\xi}\leq 2
for all
(\zeta,\xi)\in[-\frac{a}{2},\frac{a}{2}]\times[\frac{a}{2},\infty)
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\lVert u_{a}^{0}\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{a-\zeta}^{\infty}\lvert u_{0}(\xi)\rvert^{4}\xi^{m}(1+\frac{\zeta}{\xi})^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\leq 2^{\frac{m}{4}}C_{*}\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta){\mathrm{d}}\zeta=2^{\frac{m}{4}}C_{*}.

Applying the same argument to
(\rho_{a}^{0}-1,e_{a}^{0}-1)
yields the third term of
\eqref{temp:mollify2}_{2}
.

Next, by the Hölder inequality,

\displaystyle\lVert\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{2}-\lvert u_{0}\rvert^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq(1+2^{\frac{m}{4}})C_{*}\lVert u_{a}^{0}-u_{0}\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}.

From this, we now show
\lVert u_{a}^{0}-u_{0}\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\to 0
as
a\searrow 0
.
By construction (
3.6
),

\displaystyle\lVert u_{a}^{0}-u_{0}\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\chi_{a}(\hat{u}_{a}^{0}-u_{0})\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}+\Big(\int_{0}^{2a}(1-\chi_{a})^{4}\lvert u_{0}\rvert^{4}(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}.

Using the fact that
\lVert u_{0}\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq C_{*}
,
\chi_{a}\to 1
as
a\searrow 0
almost everywhere,

0\leq\chi_{a}\leq 1
, and the dominated convergence theorem, we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{a\searrow 0}\int_{0}^{2a}(1-\chi_{a})^{4}\lvert u_{0}\rvert^{4}(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r=0.

Thus,
we conclude that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{a\searrow 0}\lVert\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{2}-\lvert u_{0}\rvert^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq(1+2^{\frac{m}{4}})C_{*}\lim\limits_{a\searrow 0}\lVert\chi_{a}(\hat{u}_{a}^{0}-u_{0})\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}.

Therefore, it is left to prove the limit:

\lVert\chi_{a}(\hat{u}_{a}^{0}-u_{0})\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\to 0
as
a\searrow 0
.
To show this, it follows from the Minkowski
inequality that

\displaystyle\lVert\chi_{a}(\hat{u}_{a}^{0}-u_{0})\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{a}^{\infty}\lvert u_{0}(r-\zeta)-u_{0}(r)\rvert^{4}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{m}{4}}\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{a-\zeta}^{\infty}\lvert u_{0}(\xi)-u_{0}(\xi+\zeta)\rvert^{4}\,\xi^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta,

where we have used that
0\leq 1+\frac{\zeta}{\xi}\leq 2

if
\zeta\in[-\frac{a}{2},\frac{a}{2}]
and
\xi\geq a-\zeta
.
Now, by the separability of
space

L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
, for each
\delta>0
, there exists
h_{\delta}\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}([0,\infty))
such that

\lVert h_{\delta}-\hat{u}_{0}\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq 3^{-\frac{m}{4}}\frac{\delta}{4}
.
By the triangle inequality,
it follows that

\displaystyle 2^{-\frac{m}{4}}\lVert\chi_{a}(\hat{u}_{a}^{0}-u_{0})\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{\frac{a}{2}}^{\infty}\lvert u_{0}(\xi)-h_{\delta}(\xi)\rvert^{4}\,\xi^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{\frac{a}{2}}^{\infty}\lvert h_{\delta}(\xi)-h_{\delta}(\xi+\zeta)\rvert^{4}\,\xi^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{a-\zeta}^{\infty}\lvert h_{\delta}(\xi+\zeta)-u_{0}(\xi+\zeta)\rvert^{4}\,\xi^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta

\displaystyle=\vcentcolon\sum_{i=1}^{3}I_{i}.

First,
I_{1}
and
I_{3}
are estimated as:

\displaystyle I_{1}\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{\frac{a}{2}}^{\infty}\lvert u_{0}(\xi)-h_{\delta}(\xi)\rvert^{4}\,\xi^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\leq\dfrac{\delta}{4}\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta=\dfrac{\delta}{4},

\displaystyle I_{3}\leq

\displaystyle 3^{\frac{m}{4}}\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{a}^{\infty}\lvert h_{\delta}(r)-u_{0}(r)\rvert^{4}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\leq\dfrac{\delta}{4}\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta=\dfrac{\delta}{4},

where we have used the fact that
\frac{1}{2}\leq 1-\frac{\zeta}{r}\leq\frac{3}{2}

for all
(\zeta,r)\in[-\frac{a}{2},\frac{a}{2}]\times[a,\infty)
.
Since
h_{\delta}
is compactly supported for each
\delta>0
,
there exists
a_{\delta}\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle I_{2}\vcentcolon=\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}(\zeta)\Big(\int_{\frac{a}{2}}^{\infty}\lvert h_{\delta}(\xi)-h_{\delta}(\xi+\zeta)\rvert^{4}\,\xi^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\leq\dfrac{\delta}{2}\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}j_{a}\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta=\dfrac{\delta}{2}\qquad\text{ if $a\in(0,a_{\delta})$.}

Thus, for given
\delta>0
, there exists
a_{\delta}\in(0,1)
such that
2^{-\frac{m}{4}}\lVert\chi_{a}(\hat{u}_{a}^{0}-u_{0})\rVert_{L^{4}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq\delta
for all
a\in(0,a_{\delta})
.
This implies that

\lim_{a\searrow 0}\lVert\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{2}-\lvert u_{0}\rvert^{2}\rVert_{L^{2}([0,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}=0
.
The proof for
\rho_{a}^{0}
and
e_{a}^{0}
follows from the same argument.

Next, by the Jensen inequality and the fact
that
G(\zeta),\psi(\zeta)
, and
\lvert\zeta\rvert^{2}
are convex functions of
\zeta
,
it follows that

G(\rho_{a}^{0})\leq G(\hat{\rho}_{0})\ast j_{a}
,
\psi(e_{a}^{0})\leq\psi(\hat{e}_{0})\ast j_{a}
,
and
\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert\leq\lvert\hat{u}_{0}\rvert^{2}\ast j_{a}
for
r\in(0,\infty)
.
Using Fubini-Tonelli’s theorem, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{\infty}G(\rho_{a}^{0})(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r=\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}\Big(\int_{a-\zeta}^{\infty}G(\rho_{0})(\xi)(\zeta+\xi)^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)j_{a}(\zeta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\leq 2^{m}\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}C_{*}j_{a}(\zeta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta=2^{m}C_{*},

where we have used the initial condition
\eqref{eqs:init}_{2}
. In a similar way, we have

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{\infty}\rho_{a}^{0}\psi(e_{a}^{0})(r)r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq 2^{m}C_{*}\int_{-\frac{a}{2}}^{\frac{a}{2}}\Big(\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho_{0}\psi(e_{0})(\xi)\xi^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\Big)j_{a}(\zeta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\leq 2^{m}C_{*}^{3}.

Similarly, we also obtain

\lVert\rho_{a}^{0}\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{2}\rVert
_L^1([0,∞),r^mdr)
≤2^m C_*^3.

Setting
C_{0}=2^{m}C_{*}^{3}
,
we complete the proof.
∎

3.3.
The approximation problem in the exterior Eulerian domain

To resolve the coordinate singularity at the origin, which poses a significant difficulty
for the existence of solutions, we first consider the approximation problem
in the exterior domain:
\Omega_{a,T}\vcentcolon=[a,\infty)\times[0,T]
for fixed
a\in(0,1)
,
and find a solution
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})
of the exterior problem for system (
1.6
) over
domain
\Omega_{a,T}
with the following initial-boundary conditions:

\begin{cases}u_{a}(a,t)=0,\quad\partial_{r}e_{a}(a,t)=0\qquad&\text{for $t\in[0,T]$},\\[2.84526pt]
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})(r,0)=(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)\qquad&\text{for $r\in[a,\infty)$}.\end{cases}

(3.9)

Now the boundary conditions in (
3.9
)
can be interpreted physically as the presence of an insulating ball at the origin,
with no slip-boundary condition for the velocity field and gradient field of the internal energy. Moreover,
the initial data
are given by
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)
constructed in §
3.2
above.

To prove Theorem
2.1
, the existence of weak solutions of
the exterior problem (
1.6
) and (
3.9
) for each
a\in(0,1)
is first obtained
so that some compactness arguments
can be applied when
a\searrow 0
.

Definition 3.1

(Spherically Symmetric Weak Solutions in the Exterior Domain)
.

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{a}\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\big\{\phi\in C^{\infty}\big([a,\infty)\times[0,T]\big)\,\vcentcolon\,\exists N>a\,\ \text{such that}\ \phi(r,t)=0\ \text{for $r\geq N$}\big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}^{a}\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\big\{\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{a}\,\vcentcolon\,\phi(a,t)=0\ \text{ for all $t\in[0,T]$}\big\}.

Then
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})(r,t)
is a weak solution of problem (
1.6
) and (
3.9
),
provided that

(i)

For each
\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{a}
,

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{\infty}\rho_{a}(r,t)\phi(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r-\int_{a}^{\infty}\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\phi(r,0)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{\infty}\big(\rho_{a}\partial_{t}\phi+\rho_{a}u_{a}\partial_{r}\phi\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s.

(ii)

For each
\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{a}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{\infty}(\rho_{a}u_{a})\phi(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r-\int_{a}^{\infty}(\rho_{a}^{0}u_{a}^{0})(r)\phi(r,0)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{\infty}\rho_{a}u_{a}\big(\partial_{t}\phi+u_{a}\partial_{r}\phi\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big(P_{a}-\beta\big(\partial_{r}u_{a}+m\dfrac{u_{a}}{r}\big)\Big)\big(\partial_{r}\phi+m\dfrac{\phi}{r}\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s,

where
\beta\vcentcolon=2\mu+\lambda
.

(iii)

For each
\phi\in\mathcal{D}^{a}
,

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{\infty}(\rho_{a}E_{a})(r,t)\phi(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r-\int_{a}^{\infty}(\rho_{a}^{0}E_{a}^{0})(r)\phi(r,0)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{\infty}\big\{\rho_{a}E_{a}\partial_{t}\phi+(\rho_{a}E_{a}+P_{a})u_{a}\partial_{r}\phi\big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big\{2\mu u_{a}\partial_{r}u_{a}+\lambda u_{a}\big(\partial_{r}u_{a}+m\dfrac{u_{a}}{r}\big)+\kappa\partial_{r}e_{a}\Big\}\partial_{r}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s,

where
P_{a}\vcentcolon=(\gamma-1)\rho_{a}e_{a}
,
E_{a}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a}\rvert^{2}+e_{a}
, and
E_{a}^{0}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{2}+e_{a}^{0}
.

From now on, we denote
\sigma=\sigma(t)\vcentcolon=\min\{1,t\}
.
Moreover, for any function
y(t)\vcentcolon[0,T]\to[0,\infty)
, we denote

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}[\rho,u,e;y](T)\vcentcolon=&\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{y(t)}^{\infty}\lvert(\rho-1,u^{2},e-1,\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{r}u,\sigma\partial_{r}e)\rvert^{2}(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\\
&+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{y(t)}^{\infty}\lvert(\partial_{r}u,\partial_{r}e,u\partial_{r}u,\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{t}u,\sigma\partial_{t}e)\rvert^{2}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t.\end{split}

Theorem 3.1
.

For fixed
a\in(0,1)
, let
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)
be the initial data constructed in
Proposition
3.1
. Then, for each
T>0
, there exists a weak solution

(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})(r,t)
of the exterior problem (
1.6
) and (
3.9
).
Moreover, the weak solution
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})(r,t)
satisfies the following properties
:

(i)

The entropy inequality holds
:

\displaystyle\esssup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big(\rho_{a}\big(\frac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a}\rvert^{2}+\psi(e_{a})\big)+(\gamma-1)G(\rho_{a})\Big)(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{a}^{\infty}\dfrac{\lvert\partial_{r}e_{a}\rvert^{2}}{e_{a}^{2}}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big\{\big(\dfrac{2\mu}{n}+\lambda\big)\dfrac{\lvert\partial_{r}u_{a}+m\frac{u_{a}}{r}\rvert^{2}}{e_{a}}+\dfrac{2m\mu}{n}\dfrac{\lvert\partial_{r}u_{a}-\frac{u_{a}}{r}\rvert^{2}}{e_{a}}\Big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C(T).

(ii)

\mathcal{E}[\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a};a](T)\leq C(a)
and, for a.e.
(r,t)\in[a,\infty)\times[0,T]
,

\qquad\quad\ C^{-1}(a)\leq\rho_{a}(r,t)\leq C(a),\ \lvert u_{a}(r,t)\rvert\leq C(a)\sigma^{-\frac{1}{4}}(t),\ C^{-1}(a)\leq e_{a}(r,t)\leq C(a)\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t).

(iii)

There exists a continuous function
(x,t)\mapsto\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t)\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\times[0,T]\to[a,\infty)

such that
x\mapsto\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t)
is strictly increasing for each
t\in[0,T]
and

\begin{cases}\int_{a}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t)}\rho_{a}(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r=x&\hskip 10.22217ptfor a.e. $(x,t)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,T]$,\\
\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t)=\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}u_{a}(\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,s),s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s&\hskip 10.22217ptfor all $(x,t)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,T]$,\\
nx\psi^{-1}_{-}(\dfrac{C_{0}}{x})\leq({\widetilde{r}}_{a})^{n}(x,t)\leq C_{0}(1+x)&\hskip 10.22217ptfor all $(x,t)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,T]$,\end{cases}

where the function
x\mapsto\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[a,\infty)
is implicitly defined as

x=\int_{a}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\qquad\text{for each $x\in[0,\infty)$.}

(iv)

For each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,

\mathcal{E}[\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a};\widetilde{r}_{a}(\varepsilon,\cdot)](T)\leq C(\varepsilon)
and

\begin{cases}\lvert\widetilde{r}_{a}(x_{1},t)-\widetilde{r}_{a}(x_{2},t)\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert&\quad\text{for $(x_{1},x_{2},t)\in[\varepsilon,\infty)^{2}\times[0,T]$},\\
\lvert\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t_{1})-\widetilde{r}_{a}(x,t_{2})\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert t_{1}^{\frac{3}{4}}-t_{2}^{\frac{3}{4}}\rvert\,\,&\quad\text{for $(x,t_{1},t_{2})\in[\varepsilon,\infty)\times[0,T]^{2}$}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, for a.e.
(r,t)
such that
t\in[0,T]
and
r\in[\widetilde{r}_{a}(\varepsilon,t),\infty)
:

C^{-1}(\varepsilon)\leq\rho_{a}(r,t)\leq C(\varepsilon),\quad\lvert u_{a}(r,t)\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\sigma^{-\frac{1}{4}}(t),\quad e_{a}(r,t)\leq C(\varepsilon)\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}(t).

In addition,
for all
r_{1},\,r_{2}\in[\widetilde{r}_{a}(\varepsilon,t),\infty)
and
t\in[0,T]
,

\sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)\lvert u_{a}(r_{1},t)-u_{a}(r_{2},t)\rvert+\sigma(t)\lvert e_{a}(r_{1},t)-e_{a}(r_{2},t)\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert r_{1}-r_{2}\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}},

and, for all
(r,t_{1},t_{2})
such that
0<t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T

and
r\geq\sup_{t_{1}\leq t\leq t_{2}}\widetilde{r}_{a}(\varepsilon,t)
,

\sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(t_{1})\lvert u_{a}(r,t_{1})-u_{a}(r,t_{2})\rvert+\sigma(t_{1})\lvert e_{a}(r,t_{1})-e_{a}(r,t_{2})\rvert\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert t_{2}-t_{1}\rvert^{\frac{1}{4}}.

(v)

For each connected interval
I\subseteq[0,\infty)
, denote
\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
as the closure of

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\,\mathcal{D}_{0}(I)\vcentcolon=\big\{\phi\in C^{\infty}(I)\vcentcolon\,\exists N>0\ \text{such that $[0,N]\subset I$ and $\phi(r)=0$ for $r\in I\cap[N,\infty)$}\big\}

via the
H^{1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
–norm, and denote its dual space as
\widetilde{H}^{-1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
.
For each
\varepsilon>0
, define
\rho_{a}^{(\varepsilon)}(r,t)\vcentcolon=\rho_{a}(r,t)\chi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(r,t)

with the indicator function
\chi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(r,t)
:

\chi_{\varepsilon}^{a}(r,t)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}1&if $t\in[0,T]$ and $r\in[\widetilde{r}_{a}(\varepsilon,t),\infty)$,\\
0&otherwise.\end{cases}

Then, for all

L\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho_{a}^{(\varepsilon)}\in C^{0}\big([0,T];\widetilde{H}^{-1}([0,L],r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)\big)
and

\lVert\rho_{a}^{(\varepsilon)}(\cdot,t_{1})-\rho_{a}^{(\varepsilon)}(\cdot,t_{2})\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}([0,L],r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq C(\varepsilon)\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert\qquad\,\text{for all $t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]$.}

(vi)

For each
\eta\in(a,1)
, the following estimates hold
:

\qquad\ \begin{cases}\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{r\geq\eta}\dfrac{\lvert u_{a}\rvert}{\sqrt{e_{a}}}(r,t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C(T)\big(\eta^{\frac{2-n}{2}}+\eta^{2-n}\big)&if $n=2$, $3$,\\
\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{r\geq\eta}\log\big(\max\big\{1,e_{a}(r,t)\big\}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C(T)\big(1+\sqrt{|\log\eta|}\big)&if $n=2$,\\
\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{r\geq\eta}\log\big(\max\big\{1,e_{a}^{\pm 1}(r,t)\big\}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq C(T)\eta^{2-n}&if $n=3$.\end{cases}

Remark 3.2
.

For the smooth initial data
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})
constructed
in
§
3.2
, it can be shown that there exists a unique global-in-time classical
solution of problem (
1.6
) and (
3.9
)
(
see
[
23
]
)
. In this paper, since the higher regularity estimates for establishing classical solutions will not be needed
for the proof of the main theorem, we omit them for simplicity.

3.4.
Truncation of the initial data in the exterior Eulerian domain

Since
(\rho_{a},u_{a},e_{a})
in Theorem
3.1
is obtained as the limit of solutions
of the approximate problems posed in bounded annular domains, we need to construct the corresponding approximate initial data, which is the main purpose of this subsection.
In particular, we construct
(\rho_{a,k}^{0},u_{a,k}^{0},e_{a,k}^{0})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
by truncating
(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})
with suitable cut-off functions
\varphi_{a,k}^{0}
.

We first consider the function:

r\mapsto\,\int_{a}^{r}\rho_{a}^{0}(\zeta)\,\zeta^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta
,
which is a strictly increasing continuous function since
C_{0}^{-1}\leq\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\leq C_{0}
.
Hence, the inverse function
\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[a,\infty)
exists so that

x=\int_{a}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\,\qquad\text{for each $x\in[0,\infty)$.}

(3.10)

Moreover, due to the regularity of
\rho_{a}^{0}
, it follows from the inverse function theorem
that
\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[a,\infty)
is a smooth, bijective, strictly increasing map.

From now on, we use
{\mathrm{D}}_{x}
to represent the derivative with respect to
x\in[0,\infty)
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For all
x\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrm{D}}_{x}\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)=\dfrac{1}{(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})^{m}(x)\rho_{a}^{0}(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})(x)},\qquad\ \widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)=\Big(a^{n}+n\int_{0}^{x}\dfrac{1}{\rho_{a}^{0}(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})(y)}{\mathrm{d}}y\Big)^{\frac{1}{n}}.

(3.11)

Proof.

Since
\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\in C^{\infty}
and
C_{0}^{-1}\leq\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\leq C_{0}
by construction (
3.6
), it follows that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{r\to\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}\dfrac{1}{r-\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}\int_{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}^{r}\rho_{a}^{0}(\zeta)\,\zeta^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta=(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})^{m}(x)\rho_{a}^{0}(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x))\qquad\text{for each $x\in[0,\infty)$.}

Since

y\mapsto\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)
is continuous, then, for
all
x\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{y\to x}\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)-\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}\int_{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)}\rho_{a}^{0}(\zeta)\,\zeta^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta=(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})^{m}(x)\rho_{a}^{0}(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})(x).

Notice that, by (
3.10
),

\displaystyle\dfrac{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)-\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}{y-x}\Big(\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)-\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}\int_{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)}\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)=\dfrac{1}{y-x}\int_{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)}\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r=1.

Then, using the fact that

0<C_{0}^{-1}a^{m}\leq\rho_{0}(\widetilde{r}_{0})(y)\widetilde{r}_{0}^{\,m}(y)\leq C_{0}\widetilde{r}_{0}^{\,m}(y)<\infty
for all
y\in[0,\infty)
, it follows that, for all
x\in[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{y\to\ x}\dfrac{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)-\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}{y-x}=\lim\limits_{y\to x}\Big(\dfrac{1}{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)-\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}\int_{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(y)}\rho_{a}^{0}(r)r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})^{m}(x)\rho_{a}^{0}(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})(x)}.

Thus, the derivative of
\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)
exists and
\eqref{3,2a}_{1}
holds.

Next, it follows by the chain rule that

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{n}{\mathrm{D}}_{x}(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})^{n}(x)=(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})^{m}(x){\mathrm{D}}_{x}\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)=\frac{1}{\rho_{a}^{0}(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})(x)}\qquad\text{for $x\in[0,\infty)$.}

Integrating in
y\in[0,x]
and using
\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(0)=a
, we obtain
\eqref{3,2a}_{2}
.
The proof is complete.
∎

Now, let
\chi\vcentcolon{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
be such that
\chi\in C^{\infty}
,
\chi(\zeta)=1
if
\zeta\leq 0
,
\chi(\zeta)=0
if
\zeta\geq 1
, and
\lvert\chi^{\prime}(\zeta)\rvert\leq 2
for all
\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
With this, the cut-off functions
\varphi_{a,k}^{0}
are defined as

\varphi_{a,k}^{0}(r)=\chi\big(\dfrac{2r-\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(k)}{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(k)}\big)\qquad\,\,\text{for each $(a,k)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{N}$.}

It follows that
\varphi_{a,k}^{0}(r)\in C^{\infty}([a,\infty))
and

\varphi_{a,k}^{0}(r)=1\ \ \text{for $r\in[a,\frac{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(k)}{2}]$,}\qquad\,\,\,\varphi_{a,k}^{0}(r)=0\ \ \text{for $r\in[\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(k),\infty)$.}

Using this, we define the truncated initial data:

(\rho_{a,k}^{0},u_{a,k}^{0},e_{a,k}^{0})(r)\vcentcolon=((\rho_{a}^{0}-1)\varphi_{a,k}^{0}+1,\,u_{a}^{0}\varphi_{a,k}^{0},\,(e_{a}^{0}-1)\varphi_{a,k}^{0}+1)(r)\quad\text{ for $r\in[a,\infty)$.}

(3.12)

With above construction, it can be verified that

\begin{split}&(\rho_{a,k}^{0},u_{a,k}^{0},e_{a,k}^{0})\in C^{\infty}([a,\infty)),\qquad\ u_{a,k}^{0}(r)=\partial_{r}e_{a,k}^{0}(r)=0\,\,\,\,\text{for $r\in[a,\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(k)]$},\\[2.84526pt]
&(\rho_{a,k}^{0},u_{a,k}^{0},e_{a,k}^{0})(r)=\begin{cases}(\rho_{a}^{0},u_{a}^{0},e_{a}^{0})(r)\quad&for all $r\in[a,\frac{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(k)}{2})$,\\
(1,0,1)&for all $r\in[\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(k),\infty)$.\end{cases}\end{split}

(3.13)

Proposition 3.3
.

For
G(z)=1-z+z\log z
and
\psi(z)=z-1-\log z
,
then
following hold:

\begin{split}&C_{0}^{-1}\leq\rho_{a,k}^{0}(r)\leq C_{0},\quad e_{a,k}^{0}(r)\geq C_{0}^{-1}\qquad\ \text{ for all $(r,k)\in[a,\infty)\times\mathbb{N}$ and $a\in(0,1)$,}\\
&\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in(0,1)\\
k\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\int_{a}^{\infty}\!\!\Big\{\rho_{a,k}^{0}\big(\frac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a,k}^{0}\rvert^{2}+\psi(e_{a,k}^{0})\big)+G(\rho_{a,k}^{0})+\big|(\rho_{a,k}^{0}-1,|u_{a,k}^{0}|^{2},e_{a,k}^{0}-1)\big|^{2}\Big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C_{0}.\end{split}

Proof.

By Proposition
3.1
, we see that

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big\{\lvert\rho_{a,k}^{0}-1\rvert^{2}+\lvert u_{a,k}^{0}\rvert^{4}+\lvert e_{a,k}^{0}-1\rvert^{2}\Big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big\{\lvert\rho_{a}^{0}-1\rvert^{2}+\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{4}+\lvert e_{a}^{0}-1\rvert^{2}\Big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C_{0},

and

C_{0}^{-1}\leq\rho_{a,k}^{0}(r)=\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\varphi_{a,k}^{0}(r)+1-\varphi_{a,k}^{0}(r)\leq C_{0}

for all
r\in[a,\infty)
.
By the same argument, we can show that
e_{a,k}^{0}(r)\geq C_{0}^{-1}
for all
r\in[a,\infty)
.

Since
G
and
\psi
are convex,
G(1)=\psi(1)=0
, and
0\leq\varphi_{a,k}^{0}\leq 1
,
it follows that

\displaystyle G(\rho_{a,k}^{0})=

\displaystyle\,G(\rho_{a}^{0}\varphi_{a,k}^{0}+1-\varphi_{a,k}^{0})\leq\varphi_{a,k}^{0}G(\rho_{a}^{0})+(1-\varphi_{a,k}^{0})G(1)=\varphi_{a,k}^{0}G(\rho_{a}^{0}),

\displaystyle\psi(e_{a,k}^{0})=

\displaystyle\,\psi(e_{a}^{0}\varphi_{a,k}^{0}+1-\varphi_{a,k}^{0})\leq\varphi_{a,k}^{0}\psi(e_{a}^{0})+(1-\varphi_{a,k}^{0})\psi(1)=\varphi_{a,k}^{0}\psi(e_{a}^{0}).

Then, using Proposition
3.1
, we have

\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big\{\rho_{a,k}^{0}\big(\frac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a,k}^{0}\rvert^{2}+\psi(e_{a,k}^{0})\big)+G(\rho_{a,k}^{0})\Big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C_{0}\int_{a}^{\infty}\Big\{\rho_{a}^{0}\big(\dfrac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a}^{0}\rvert^{2}+\psi(e_{a}^{0})\big)+G(\rho_{a}^{0})\Big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C_{0}.

This completes the proof.
∎

3.5.
Reformulation in the bounded Lagrangian domain

For each
(a,k)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{N}
, we transform
(\rho_{a,k}^{0},u_{a,k}^{0},e_{a,k}^{0})(r)
into the Lagrangian domain as

(\widetilde{v}_{a,k}^{0},\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{0},\widetilde{e}_{a,k}^{0})(x)\vcentcolon=\big((\rho_{a,k}^{0})^{-1},u_{a,k}^{0},e_{a,k}^{0}\big)(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x))\qquad\,\text{for $x\in[0,\infty)$.}

(3.14)

Since
\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty))
due to
\rho_{a}^{0}\in C^{\infty}([a,\infty))
, Proposition
3.2
and (
3.13
) imply that

(\widetilde{v}_{a,k}^{0},\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{0},\widetilde{e}_{a,k}^{0})\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty)),\,\,\,\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{0}(x)\big|_{x=0,\,k}={\mathrm{D}}_{x}\widetilde{e}_{a,k}^{0}(x)\big|_{x=0,\,k}=0\qquad\text{ for $(a,k)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{N}$.}

Proposition 3.4
.

The following uniform estimate holds
:

\sup_{a\in(0,1)}\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{k}\Big(\dfrac{1}{2}\lvert\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{0}\rvert^{2}+\psi(\widetilde{e}_{a,k}^{0})+\psi(\widetilde{v}_{a,k}^{0})+\lvert(\widetilde{v}_{a,k}^{0}-1,|\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{0}|^{2},\widetilde{e}_{a,k}^{0}-1)\rvert^{2}\Big)(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq C_{0}.

Proof.

First, it follows from Proposition
3.2
that the map:
x\mapsto\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)
satisfies

0<C_{0}^{-1}\,(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})^{-m}(x)\leq\lvert{\mathrm{D}}_{x}\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)\rvert=(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})^{-m}(x)\,(\rho_{a}^{0})^{-1}(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x))\leq C_{0}a^{-m}<\infty\qquad\text{for $x\in[0,\infty)$,}

so that the Jacobian of transformation
r=\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)
is bounded.
Then, for any function
f(r)\vcentcolon[a,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}
, the following coordinate transformation is satisfied:

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(k)}f(r)\rho_{a}^{0}(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r=\int_{0}^{k}f(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0})(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

Using this, (
3.13
), and Propositions
3.1
–
3.3
,
it follows that, for each
(a,k)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{k}\lvert\widetilde{v}_{a,k}^{0}-1\rvert^{2}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{0}^{k}\left\lvert 1-\rho_{a,k}^{0}\big(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)\big)\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\rho_{a,k}^{0}\big(\widetilde{r}_{a}^{0}(x)\big)\right\rvert^{-2}{\mathrm{d}}x\leq C_{0}\int_{a}^{\infty}\left\lvert\rho_{a}^{0}(r)-1\right\rvert^{2}r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C_{0}.

By the same argument,

\lVert(\lvert\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{0}\rvert^{2},\widetilde{e}_{a,k}^{0}-1)\rVert_{L^{2}([a,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq C_{0}
.

Next, by Propositions
3.1
–
3.3

and the identity:
z\psi(z^{-1})=1-z+z\log z=G(z)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{k}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\lvert\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{0}\rvert^{2}+\psi(\widetilde{e}_{a,k}^{0})+(\gamma-1)\psi(\widetilde{v}_{a,k}^{0})\Big)(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{\infty}\dfrac{\rho_{a}^{0}}{\rho_{a,k}^{0}}\Big(\rho_{a,k}^{0}\big(\frac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a,k}^{0}\rvert^{2}+\psi(e_{a,k}^{0})\big)+(\gamma-1)G(\rho_{a,k}^{0})\Big)(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C_{0}

for all
(a,k)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{N}
. This completes the proof.
∎

The vector function
(\widetilde{v}_{a,k},\widetilde{u}_{a,k},\widetilde{e}_{a,k})
is called a solution of the
(a,k)
-approximate IBVP in the Lagrangian coordinates,
provided that
(\widetilde{v}_{a,k},\widetilde{u}_{a,k},\widetilde{e}_{a,k})
satisfies the equations:

\begin{cases}{\mathrm{D}}_{t}\widetilde{v}_{a,k}-{\mathrm{D}}_{x}(\widetilde{r}_{a,k}^{m}\widetilde{u}_{a,k})=0,\\
{\mathrm{D}}_{t}\widetilde{u}_{a,k}+\widetilde{r}_{a,k}^{m}{\mathrm{D}}_{x}p(\widetilde{v}_{a,k},\widetilde{e}_{a,k})=\beta\widetilde{r}_{a,k}^{m}{\mathrm{D}}_{x}\Big(\dfrac{{\mathrm{D}}_{x}(\widetilde{r}_{a,k}^{m}\widetilde{u}_{a,k})}{\widetilde{v}_{a,k}}\Big),\\
{\mathrm{D}}_{t}\widetilde{e}_{a,k}+p(\widetilde{v}_{a,k},\widetilde{e}_{a,k}){\mathrm{D}}_{x}(\widetilde{r}_{a,k}^{m}\widetilde{u}_{a,k})=\mathcal{G}_{a,k},\\
\widetilde{r}_{a,k}(x,t)=\Big(a^{n}+n\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\widetilde{v}_{a,k}(y,t){\mathrm{d}}y\Big)^{\frac{1}{n}}\end{cases}

(3.15)

in
[0,k]\times[0,T]
with
p(v,e)\vcentcolon=\frac{(\gamma-1)e}{v}
,
\beta\vcentcolon=2\mu+\lambda
, and

\mathcal{G}_{a,k}\vcentcolon=\beta\dfrac{\lvert{\mathrm{D}}_{x}(\widetilde{r}_{a,k}^{m}\widetilde{u}_{a,k})\rvert^{2}}{\widetilde{v}_{a,k}}-2m\mu{\mathrm{D}}_{x}(\widetilde{r}_{a,k}^{m-1}\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{2})+\kappa{\mathrm{D}}_{x}\Big(\dfrac{r_{a,k}^{2m}{\mathrm{D}}_{x}\widetilde{e}_{a,k}}{\widetilde{v}_{a,k}}\Big),

and the initial-boundary conditions:

\begin{cases}\widetilde{u}_{a,k}(x,t)\big|_{x=0,\,k}={\mathrm{D}}_{x}\widetilde{e}_{a,k}(x,t)\big|_{x=0,\,k}=0&\text{for $t\in[0,T]$},\\
(\widetilde{v}_{a,k},\widetilde{u}_{a,k},\widetilde{e}_{a,k})(x,0)=(\widetilde{v}_{a,k}^{0},\widetilde{u}_{a,k}^{0},\widetilde{e}_{a,k}^{0})(x)\qquad&\text{for $x\in[0,k]$},\end{cases}

(3.16)

Remark 3.3
.

The equations in (
3.15
) are formally derived
from system (
1.6
) and (
3.9
) as follows
:
for any smooth solution
(\rho,u,e)
of (
1.6
) and (
3.9
), the transformation
from the Eulerian coordinates
(r,t)
to the Lagrangian coordinates
(x,t)
is defined as

x(r,t)\vcentcolon=\int_{a}^{r}\rho(s,t)\,s^{m}{\mathrm{d}}s,\quad t(r,t)\vcentcolon=t\qquad\text{ for $(r,t)\in[a,\infty)\times[0,T]$}.

(3.17)

The continuity equation
\eqref{eqs:SFNS}_{1}
implies that

\partial_{t}x(r,t)=-\rho ur^{m}
.
In addition, by the implicit function theorem,
on a neighbourhood where
\rho>0
, there is a function
r(x,t)
such that

\int_{a}^{r(x,t)}\rho(\zeta,t)\,\zeta^{m}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta=x.

(3.18)

Denote
(v,u,e)(x,t):=(\rho^{-1}(r(x,t),t),u(r(x,t),t),e(r(x,t),t))

and
({\mathrm{D}}_{t},{\mathrm{D}}_{x})
as the derivative with respect to the Lagrangian coordinates.
Then it follows by taking derivative
{\mathrm{D}}_{t}
on
(
3.18
)
and the Leibniz theorem
that
{\mathrm{D}}_{t}r(x,t)=u(x,t)
. These relations can be summarized as

\dfrac{\partial(x,t)}{\partial(r,t)}=\begin{pmatrix}\rho r^{m}&-\rho ur^{m}\\[2.84526pt]
0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad\dfrac{\partial(r,t)}{\partial(x,t)}=\begin{pmatrix}vr^{-m}&u\\[2.84526pt]
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(3.19)

Furthermore, from the continuity equation
\eqref{eqs:SFNS}_{1}
,
