\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\qquad\,\,\mbox{as $j\to\infty$}.

By the same argument, we also obtain

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a_{j}}^{\infty}\big\{\rho_{a_{j}}E_{a_{j}}\partial_{t}\phi+(\rho_{a_{j}}E_{a_{j}}+P_{a_{j}})u_{a_{j}}\partial_{r}\phi\big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}\big\{\rho E\partial_{t}\phi+(\rho E+P)u\partial_{r}\phi\big\}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s.

Next, integrating by parts and using Lemma
6.3
, we obtain that, for all
j\geq N
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a_{j}}^{\infty}u_{a_{j}}\partial_{r}u_{a_{j}}\partial_{r}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s=-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\widetilde{r}(2\varepsilon,s)}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{2}\lvert u_{a_{j}}\rvert^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s\to-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\widetilde{r}(2\varepsilon,s)}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{2}\lvert u\rvert^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s,

as
j\to\infty
.
From
(
6.13
), the spatial weak derivative of
u
exists with

\partial_{r}u\in L^{2}_{\text{loc}}(\mathrm{F})
and
\partial_{r}u\in L^{2}(\widetilde{\mathrm{F}}_{\varepsilon})
for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
It then follows that, as
j\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a_{j}}^{\infty}u_{a_{j}}\partial_{r}u_{a_{j}}\partial_{r}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s\to-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\widetilde{r}(2\varepsilon,s)}^{\infty}\dfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}u\partial_{r}u\partial_{r}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s,

where we have used
{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)\Subset\mathrm{F}
.
By the same argument, we also have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{a_{j}}^{\infty}\Big\{m\lambda\dfrac{\lvert u_{a_{j}}\rvert^{2}}{r}+\kappa\partial_{r}e_{a_{j}}\Big\}\partial_{r}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}\Big\{m\lambda\dfrac{\lvert u\rvert^{2}}{r}+\kappa\partial_{r}e\Big\}\partial_{r}\phi\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s.

Combining all the limits established above with (
6.34
), we obtain Lemma
6.9
.
∎

Finally, the weak forms obtained in Theorem
6.8
–
6.9

are translated into the Eulerian coordinates
({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]
.
Let
\Phi\in C^{1}([0,T];C_{c}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}))
.
Define

\displaystyle\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\vcentcolon=\rho(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert,t),\quad{\boldsymbol{U}}({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\vcentcolon=u(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert,t)\frac{{\boldsymbol{x}}}{r},\qquad\,\phi(r,t)\vcentcolon=\int_{S^{n-1}}\Phi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},t){\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}},

where
S^{n-1}
is the
(n-1)
-dimensional sphere,
and
{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}
is the surface measure on
S^{n-1}
.
Using this, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\rho u)(r,s)\partial_{r}\phi(r,s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{S^{n-1}}(\rho u)(r,s){\boldsymbol{y}}\cdot\nabla\Phi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(\rho u)(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert,s)\dfrac{{\boldsymbol{x}}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}\cdot\nabla\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},s){\boldsymbol{U}}({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\cdot\nabla\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s.

By the same way, we also have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho(r,s)\phi(r,s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big|_{s=0}^{s=t}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},0)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}},

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho(r,s)\partial_{t}\phi(r,s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\rho({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\partial_{t}\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s.

Since
\phi(r,t)
satisfies the assumption in Lemma
6.8
,
using the above identities in Lemma
6.8
,
we obtain the weak form of the continuity equation.

Next, we show the momentum equations.
Let
\Psi\vcentcolon{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
be such that

\Psi\in C^{2}([0,T];C^{2}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}))
and

{\mathrm{supp}}(\Psi)\Subset\mathcal{F}\vcentcolon=\{({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]\,\vcentcolon\,\underline{r}(t)<\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\}
. We define

\varphi^{i}(r,t)\vcentcolon=\int_{S^{n-1}}\Psi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},t)y^{i}\,{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\quad\,\,\,\text{for $i=1,\dotsc,n$.}

(6.35)

It can be verified that
\varphi^{i}
satisfies the assumption for Lemma
6.9
,
hence (
6.32
) holds with
\varphi^{i}
.
The derivations for the weak forms in Theorem
2.1
are similar to that occurring
in the continuity equation, except for the term:

\iint\{P-\beta(\partial_{r}u+m\frac{u}{r})\}(\partial_{r}\varphi^{i}+m\frac{\varphi^{i}}{r})\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s
.
To deal with this, we use the following identity that can be verified with few lines of calculation
on the spherical coordinate transformation:

\int_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert\leq r}\partial_{x^{i}}f({\boldsymbol{x}})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}=\int_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert=r}f({\boldsymbol{x}})\dfrac{x^{i}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}\,{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\qquad\ \text{for $i=1,\dotsc,n$, for $r>0$.}

Using this identity, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(r^{m}\varphi^{i})

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}\Big(\int_{S^{n-1}}y^{i}\Psi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\Big)=\partial_{r}\Big(\int_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert=r}\dfrac{x^{i}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}\Psi({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\partial_{r}\Big(\int_{0}^{r}\int_{S^{n-1}}\partial_{x^{i}}\Psi(\zeta{\boldsymbol{y}},t)\,\zeta^{m}{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\Big)=r^{m}\int_{S^{n-1}}\partial_{x^{i}}\Psi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}.

Take the derivative on (
6.35
) to obtain that
\partial_{r}\varphi^{i}(r,t)=\int_{S^{n-1}}y^{i}y^{j}\partial_{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{j}}\Psi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},t){\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}
so that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}P\big(\partial_{r}\varphi^{i}+\dfrac{m}{r}\varphi^{i}\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{S^{n-1}}P\partial_{x^{i}}\Psi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}P\partial_{x^{i}}\Psi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s.

Moreover, integrating by parts yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(\partial_{r}u+m\dfrac{u}{r}\big)\big(\partial_{r}\varphi^{i}+m\dfrac{\varphi^{i}}{r}\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(\partial_{r}u\partial_{r}\varphi^{i}+m\dfrac{\varphi^{i}u}{r^{2}}\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t.

Using the identity
\partial_{x^{j}}{\boldsymbol{U}}^{i}=\partial_{r}(\frac{u}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert})\frac{x^{i}x^{j}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}+\frac{u}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}\delta^{ij}
,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\partial_{x^{j}}{\boldsymbol{U}}^{i}\partial_{x^{j}}\Psi{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\Big(\partial_{r}\big(\dfrac{u}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}\big)\dfrac{x^{i}x^{j}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}+\dfrac{u}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}\delta^{ij}\Big)\partial_{x^{j}}\Psi{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\Big(r\partial_{r}\big(\dfrac{u}{r}\big)y^{i}y^{j}\partial_{x^{j}}\Psi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},t)+\dfrac{u}{r}\partial_{x^{i}}\Psi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},t)\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(r^{m+1}\partial_{r}\big(\dfrac{u}{r}\big)\partial_{r}\varphi^{i}+\dfrac{u}{r}\partial_{r}(r^{m}\varphi^{i})\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(\partial_{r}u+m\dfrac{u}{r}\big)\big(\partial_{r}\varphi^{i}+m\dfrac{\varphi^{i}}{r}\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t.

Similarly, since
{\mathrm{div}}\,{\boldsymbol{U}}=\partial_{r}u+m\frac{u}{r}
, we also have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}{\mathrm{div}}{\boldsymbol{U}}\partial_{x^{i}}\Psi\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(\partial_{r}u+m\dfrac{u}{r}\big)\big(\partial_{r}\varphi^{i}+m\dfrac{\varphi^{i}}{r}\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t.

Finally, we show the weak form of energy equation. Let
\varPhi\in C^{2}([0,T];C^{2}_{\rm c}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}))

be a test function such that

{\mathrm{supp}}(\Phi)\Subset\mathcal{F}=\{({\boldsymbol{x}},t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times[0,T]\,\vcentcolon\,\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert>\underline{r}(t)\}
.
Define

\displaystyle\phi(r,t)\vcentcolon=\int_{S^{n-1}}\Phi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},t)\,{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}.

It can be verified that
\phi
satisfies the assumption of Lemma
6.9
, so that
(
6.33
) holds with
\phi
.
Most of the terms can be derived by the same procedure presented previously,
except for the terms:
u\partial_{r}u
,
\frac{u^{2}}{r}
, and
\partial_{r}e
.
Since
{\mathrm{supp}}(\phi)\Subset\{(r,t)\,\vcentcolon\,r>\underline{r}(t)\}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}u\partial_{r}u\partial_{r}\phi(r,s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}u\partial_{r}u\Big(\int_{S^{n-1}}y^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}\Phi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},s){\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}\Big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert>\underline{r}(s)}\partial_{r}\big(\dfrac{u^{2}}{2}\big)(\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert,s)\dfrac{x^{i}}{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert}\partial_{x^{i}}\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert>\underline{r}(s)}\partial_{x^{i}}\big(\dfrac{\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}\rvert^{2}}{2}\big)\partial_{x^{i}}\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert>\underline{r}(s)}\nabla\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\cdot\nabla\lvert{\boldsymbol{U}}\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s,

and with several lines of derivations, we also have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}\big\{\mu u\partial_{r}u+\lambda u\big(\partial_{r}u+m\dfrac{u}{r}\big)\big\}\partial_{r}\phi(r,s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}\int_{S^{n-1}}\big\{\mu u\partial_{r}u+\lambda u\big(\partial_{r}u+m\dfrac{u}{r}\big)\big\}y^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}\Phi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert>\underline{r}(s)}\{\lambda({\mathrm{div}}{\boldsymbol{U}}){\boldsymbol{U}}+\mu(\nabla{\boldsymbol{U}})\cdot{\boldsymbol{U}}\}\nabla\Phi({\boldsymbol{x}},s)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\mathrm{d}}s,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}\partial_{r}e\,\partial_{r}\phi(r,s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\underline{r}(s)}^{\infty}\int_{S^{n-1}}\partial_{r}e(r,s)y^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}\Phi(r{\boldsymbol{y}},s)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}S_{{\boldsymbol{y}}}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\lvert{\boldsymbol{x}}\rvert>\underline{r}(s)}\nabla e\cdot\nabla\Phi\,{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s.

With these, we complete the proof of the weak form of the energy equation.

Appendix A
Some Variants of the Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem

This appendix is devoted to the proof of two variants of the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem,
which are used in §
5.3
–§
5.4
for the limit process
as
k\to\infty

and in §
6.2
–§
6.3
for the limit process as
a\searrow 0
.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a
domain with Lipschitz boundary
such that
\text{Int}(D)\neq\emptyset
,
where
Int(\cdot)
denotes the set of interior points.
Assume that
\{f_{i}\vcentcolon D\to\mathbb{R}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a collection of functions,
and
\{(D_{\alpha},C_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
is a collection of compact subsets

D_{\alpha}\Subset D
and constants
C_{\alpha}\in(0,\infty)
such that,
for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}\subseteq D_{\alpha+1},\quad\text{Int}(D_{\alpha})\neq\emptyset,\quad D=\cup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}D_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sup\limits_{x\in D_{\alpha}}f_{i}(x)\leq C_{\alpha},\qquad\,\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(y)\rvert\leq C_{\alpha}\lvert x-y\rvert\quad\text{for all $x,y\in D_{\alpha}$}.

(A.1)

Then there exist both a continuous function
f\vcentcolon D\to\mathbb{R}

and a subsequence
\{i_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with
i_{j}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
such that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\sup_{x\in K}\lvert f_{i_{j}}(x)-f(x)\rvert=0\ \

\displaystyle\text{for all compact subset $K\Subset D$,}

\displaystyle\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert\leq C_{\alpha}\lvert x-y\rvert\ \

\displaystyle\text{for all $x,y\in D_{\alpha}$, for each $\alpha\in\mathbb{N}$.}

Proof.

We split the proof into three steps: the desired subsequence, the limit function,
and the uniform convergence and continuity.

1.
The desired subsequence
.
By the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, it follows that, for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exist both a continuous function
f^{(\alpha)}(x,t)\vcentcolon D_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{R}
and a subsequence
\{i_{j}^{(\alpha)}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
i_{j}^{(\alpha)}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
, and

\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\sup_{x\in D_{\alpha}}\lvert f^{(\alpha)}(x)-f_{i_{j}^{(\alpha)}}(x)\rvert=0.

(A.2)

Based on (
A.2
), one can inductively construct a chain of subsequences

\{i_{j}^{(\alpha+1)}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{i_{j}^{(\alpha)}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then taking the diagonal subsequence
i_{j}\vcentcolon=i_{j}^{(j)}
yields that, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,

\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\sup_{x\in D_{\alpha}}\lvert f^{(\alpha)}(x)-f_{i_{j}}(x)\rvert=0.

(A.3)

If
x\in D_{\alpha}
for some integer
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
, then, for any other integer
q\geq\alpha+1
,
we see that, for all
x\in D_{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\lvert f^{(\alpha)}(x)-f^{(q)}(x)\rvert\leq\lvert f^{(\alpha)}(x)-f_{i_{j}}(x)\rvert+\lvert f_{i_{j}}(x)-f^{(q)}(x)\rvert\to 0\qquad\text{as $j\to\infty$}.

Thus, if
q\geq\alpha
are two integers, then

f^{(q)}(x)=f^{(\alpha)}(x)\qquad\text{ for all $x\in D_{\alpha}$}.

(A.4)

2.
The limit function
. For each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,

f^{(\alpha)}(x)
is extended to

D
by

h^{(\alpha)}(x)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}f^{(\alpha)}(x)&if $x\in D_{\alpha}$,\\
0&if $x\in D\backslash D_{\alpha}$.\end{cases}

With this, we can define the following function
g_{N}(x)
in

D
:

\displaystyle g_{N}(x)\vcentcolon=h^{(1)}(x)+\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\{h^{(k+1)}(x)-h^{(k)}(x)\}\qquad\ \text{for each $N\in\mathbb{N}$ and $N\geq 2$}.

Then, for a fixed point
y\in D
, there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
such that
y\in D_{\alpha}
and
y\notin D_{\alpha-1}
,
with
D_{0}\vcentcolon=\emptyset
.
By (
A.4
) and the assumption that
D_{q}\subseteq D_{q+1}
for each
q\in\mathbb{N}
,
it follows that
h^{(q)}(y)=0
if
q\leq\alpha-1
, and
h^{(q)}(y)=h^{(\alpha)}(y)
if
q\geq\alpha
.
Hence, it follows that, for all
N\geq\alpha+1
,

\displaystyle g_{N}(y)=h^{(1)}(y)+\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\{h^{(k+1)}(y)-h^{(k)}(y)\}=h^{(N)}(y)=h^{(\alpha)}(y)=f^{(\alpha)}(y).

Thus,
\lim_{N\to\infty}g_{N}(y)=f^{(\alpha)}(y)
for this point
y\in D
.
Since this is true for any arbitrary point
x\in D
, we obtain that

f(x)\vcentcolon D\to\mathbb{R}
, given by

f(x)\vcentcolon=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}g_{N}(x),

is well-defined and, in particular,
f(x)=f^{(\alpha)}(x)
if
x\in D_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
.
By the uniform convergence (
A.3
), it follows that, for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,

\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\sup_{x\in D_{\alpha}}\lvert f(x)-f_{i_{j}}(x)\rvert=\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\sup_{x\in D_{\alpha}}\lvert f^{(\alpha)}(x)-f_{i_{j}}(x)\rvert=0.

(A.5)

3.
The uniform convergence and continuity
.
Let
K\Subset D
be any compact subset.
Then
\{\text{Int}(D_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}
is an open covering for
K
, so that
there exists a finite subcovering
\{D_{\alpha_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{N}
of
K
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Notice that
D_{q}\subseteq D_{q+1}
for each
q\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore,
there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
such that
K\subseteq D_{\alpha}
.
Combining with (
A.5
), it follows that

\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}\sup_{x\in K}\lvert f_{i_{j}}(x)-f(x)\rvert=0.

For the continuity of
f
, fixing a point
x\in D
, then there exists
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
such that
x\in D_{\alpha}
.
Since
\text{Int}(D_{\alpha})\neq\emptyset
,
there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that
B_{\varepsilon_{1}}(x)\subset D_{\alpha}
.
According to (
A.1
),

\sup\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert f_{i}(x)-f_{i}(y)\rvert\leq C_{\alpha}\lvert x-y\rvert\qquad\text{for all $y\in D_{\alpha}$}.

Applying the uniform convergence (
A.5
), it follows that
\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert\leq C_{\alpha}\lvert x-y\rvert

for all
y\in D_{\alpha}
.
Given
\varepsilon>0
, define
\delta\vcentcolon=\min\{\varepsilon_{1},\frac{\varepsilon}{C_{\alpha}}\}>0
.
Then, for
y\in B_{\delta}(x)\subseteq B_{\varepsilon_{1}}(x)\subset D_{\alpha}
,

\lvert f(x)-f(y)\rvert\leq C_{\alpha}\lvert x-y\rvert\leq\varepsilon.

The continuity of
x\mapsto f(x)
has been proved.
∎

Before stating the next proposition,
we define
the functional space
\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
for each connected interval
I\subseteq[0,\infty)

as the closure of

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}(I)\vcentcolon=\big\{\phi\in C^{\infty}(I)\,\vcentcolon\,\exists N>a\text{ such that }[a,N]\subset I\text{ and }\phi(r)=0\text{ for }r\in I\cap[N,\infty)\big\}

via the
H^{1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
–norm.
We also denote its dual space as
\widetilde{H}^{-1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
.
Then it follows that
\widetilde{H}^{-1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
endowed with the norm:

\lVert f\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\vcentcolon=\sup\big\{\langle f,g\rangle_{\widetilde{H}^{-1},\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1}}\,\vcentcolon\,\lVert g\rVert_{\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1}(I,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq 1\big\}

is a Banach space.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
I=[a,b]
be an interval for some
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
and let
D=[d,\infty)
be an unbounded interval with some fixed
d\in(0,1)
.
Suppose that
\{f_{i}(r,t)\,\vcentcolon\,D\times I\to\mathbb{R}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}

is a collection of functions satisfying
f_{i}(r,t)\in L^{\infty}(I;\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))

for each
i,L\in\mathbb{N}
, where
D_{L}\vcentcolon=D\cap[0,L]=[d,L]
.
In addition, suppose that there exists a constant
C>0
for which the following bounds
are satisfied
:
for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in I
,

\begin{split}&\sup\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert f_{i}(\cdot,t_{2})-f_{i}(\cdot,t_{1})\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq C\lvert t_{2}-t_{1}\rvert\qquad\text{for all $L\in\mathbb{N}$},\\
&\sup\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sup\limits_{t\in I}\int_{D}\lvert f_{i}-1\rvert^{2}(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq C.\end{split}

(A.6)

Then there exist both a subsequence
\{i_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and a function
f
such that, for all
L\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle f-1\in L^{\infty}(I;L^{2}(D,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)),\quad\ f\in C^{0}\big(I;\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)\big),

(A.7)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in I}\lVert f_{i_{k}}(\cdot,t)-f(\cdot,t)\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}=0,

\displaystyle\lVert f(\cdot,t_{1})-f(\cdot,t_{2})\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq C\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert\qquad\text{ for all $t_{1}$, $t_{2}\in I$},

where

C>0
is independent of
L\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof in four steps:
the desired subsequence, the desired Cauchy sequence, the uniform convergence,
and the independence of domain size.

1.
The desired subsequence
.
Fix a large integer
L\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{D_{L}}\lvert f_{i}\rvert^{2}{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle\leq 2d^{-m}\int_{D_{L}}\lvert f_{i}-1\rvert^{2}r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r+2L\leq 2d^{-m}C+2L<\infty.

Hence, the following uniform estimate hold:

\sup\limits_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sup\limits_{t\in I}\int_{D_{L}}\lvert f_{i}\rvert^{2}(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq 2d^{-m}C+2L<\infty.

(A.8)

Let
\{t_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset I
be a countable dense subset.
Then, from (
A.8
), for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exist both a function
g_{j,\,L}\in L^{2}(D_{L},{\mathrm{d}}r)
and
a subsequence
\{i^{(j)}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

f_{i_{k}^{(j)}}(\cdot,t_{j})\rightharpoonup g_{j,\,L}(\cdot)

weakly as
k\to\infty
in
L^{2}(D_{L},{\mathrm{d}}r)
.
Thus, one can inductively obtain a chain of subsequence

\{i_{k}^{(j+1)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{i_{k}^{(j)}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Taking the diagonal sequence
i_{k}\vcentcolon=i_{k}^{(k)}
,
then, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

f_{i_{k}}(\cdot,t_{j})\rightharpoonup g_{j,L}(\cdot)\qquad\text{weakly as $k\to\infty$ in $L^{2}(D_{L},{\mathrm{d}}r)$}.

(A.9)

2.
The desired Cauchy sequence
. Define

M^{i}(r,t)\vcentcolon=\int_{D\cap[0,r]}f_{i}(\zeta,t)\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\qquad\ \text{for each $r\leq L$}.

Then it follows from (
A.9
) that, for each
(r,j)\in[0,L]\times\mathbb{N}
,

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}M^{i_{k}}(r,t_{j})=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\int_{D\cap[0,r]}f_{i_{k}}(\zeta,t_{j})\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta\longrightarrow\int_{D\cap[0,r]}g_{j,\,L}(\zeta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\zeta=\vcentcolon M_{j,\,L}(r).

(A.10)

For all
\phi\in\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{D_{L}}(f_{i_{k}}(r,t_{j})-g_{j}(r))\phi(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle=\int_{D_{L}}\partial_{r}\big(M^{i_{k}}(r,t_{j})-M_{j,\,L}(r)\big)\phi(r)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle=-\int_{D_{L}}\big(M^{i_{k}}(r,t_{j})-M_{j,L}(r)\big)\big(\partial_{r}\phi+m\dfrac{\phi}{r}\big)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{C}{d}\lVert\phi\rVert_{H^{1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\Big(\int_{D_{L}}\lvert M^{i_{k}}(r,t_{j})-M_{j,\,L}(r)\rvert^{2}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Therefore, by (
A.10
) and the dominated convergence theorem,
we see that, for
j\in\mathbb{N}
,

\lVert f_{i_{k}}(\cdot,t_{j})-g_{j}(\cdot)\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq\dfrac{C}{d}\Big(\int_{D_{L}}\lvert M^{i_{k}}(r,t_{j})-M_{j,\,L}(r)\rvert^{2}\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\to 0\qquad\mbox{as $k\to\infty$}.

In particular, since
L\in\mathbb{N}
can be arbitrarily chosen, the following statement holds:

\{f_{i_{k}}(\cdot,t_{j})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,\,\,\text{ is a Cauchy sequence in }\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)\text{ for each }(j,L)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}.

(A.11)

3.
The uniform convergence
.
Given
\delta>0
, define
I_{j}\vcentcolon=(t_{j}-\frac{\delta}{3C},t_{j}+\frac{\delta}{3C})
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
t_{j}\in I_{j}
and

\sup\limits_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\lVert f_{i_{k}}(\cdot,t)-f_{i_{k}}(\cdot,t_{j})\right\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq C\lvert t-t_{j}\rvert\leq\dfrac{\delta}{3}\qquad\ \text{for all $t\in I_{j}$}.

(A.12)

Since
\{t_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset I
is dense,

\{I_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is an open covering of
I=[a,b]
.
It follows that there exists a finite subcovering
\{I_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}
for some
M\in\mathbb{N}
.
Now, for each
j=1,\dotsc,M
, it follows from (
A.11
) that
there exists
N_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\lVert f_{i_{p}}(\cdot,t_{j})-f_{i_{q}}(\cdot,t_{j})\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq\dfrac{\delta}{3}\qquad\ \text{for all $p,q\geq N_{j}$}.

(A.13)

Set
N\vcentcolon=\max\{N_{j}\vcentcolon j=1,\dotsc,M\}
. Then, for all
t\in I
,
there exists
j\in\{1,\dotsc,M\}
such that
t\in I_{j}
so that,
by (
A.12
)–(
A.13
), for all
p,q\geq N
,

\displaystyle\lVert f_{i_{p}}(\cdot,t)-f_{i_{q}}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert f_{i_{p}}(\cdot,t)-f_{i_{p}}(\cdot,t_{j})\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}+\lVert f_{i_{p}}(\cdot,t_{j})-f_{i_{q}}(\cdot,t_{j})\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}

\displaystyle\quad+\lVert f_{i_{q}}(\cdot,t_{j})-f_{i_{q}}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq\delta.

Since this is true for any
t\in I
,

\sup\limits_{t\in I}\lVert f_{i_{p}}(\cdot,t)-f_{i_{q}}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq\delta\qquad\ \text{for all $p,q\geq N$}.

Therefore,
\{f_{i_{k}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence
in
L^{\infty}(I;\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
, which is a Banach space.
Then there exists
g_{L}\in L^{\infty}(I;\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
such that

\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\sup\limits_{t\in I}\lVert f_{i_{k}}(\cdot,t)-g_{L}(\cdot,t)\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}=0.

(A.14)

It also follows from the Lipschitz continuity assumption
and (
A.14
) that

\lVert g_{L}(\cdot,t_{1})-g_{L}(\cdot,t_{2})\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)}\leq C\lvert t_{1}-t_{2}\rvert\qquad\text{for all $t_{1},t_{2}\in I$},

which implies that
g_{L}\in C^{0}(I;\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
.

4.
The independence of domain size
.
We claim there exists
f-1\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(D,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
, independent of
L\in\mathbb{N}
,
such that
f=g_{L}
in
L^{\infty}(I,\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
.

It follows from (
A.6
) that there exist both a function
f-1\in L^{\infty}(I;L^{2}(D,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))

and a subsequence (still denoted)
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

f_{i}-1\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}f-1\qquad\text{in $L^{\infty}\big(I;L^{2}(D,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)\big)$ \, as $i\to\infty$}.

(A.15)

Let
\varphi\in L^{1}(I,\widetilde{H}^{1}_{0}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
,
and let
\{i_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the subsequence in (
A.14
).
Then, using (
A.14
)–(
A.15
), it follows that

\displaystyle\Big\lvert\int_{I}\langle f-g_{L},\varphi\rangle_{\widetilde{H}^{-1},\,\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1}}{\mathrm{d}}t\Big\rvert\leq\Big\lvert\int_{I}\langle f-f_{i_{k}},\varphi\rangle_{\widetilde{H}^{-1},\,\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1}}{\mathrm{d}}t\Big\rvert+\Big\lvert\int_{I}\langle f_{i_{k}}-g_{L},\varphi\rangle_{\widetilde{H}^{-1},\,\widetilde{H}_{0}^{1}}{\mathrm{d}}t\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\Big\lvert\int_{I}\int_{D_{L}}(f-f_{i_{k}})(r,t)\varphi(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t\Big\rvert+\int_{I}\lVert f_{i_{k}}-g_{L}\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{-1}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\widetilde{H}^{1}_{0}}{\mathrm{d}}t\to 0\qquad\mbox{as $k\to\infty$},

which implies that
f=g_{L}
in
L^{\infty}(I,\widetilde{H}^{-1}(D_{L},r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
.
Since
f
is independent of
L\in\mathbb{N}
, we conclude from (
A.14
)
that (
A.7
) holds for all
L\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Appendix B
Some Properties of Convex Functions

This appendix is devoted to disclosing some properties of convex functions,
which have been used in §
5.5
, §
6.1
, and §
6.3
.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
G(\cdot)
,
\psi(\cdot)
, and
H(\cdot)
be the convex functions defined in (
6.1
).
If
G^{-1}_{+}
,
\psi_{+}^{-1}
, and
H_{+}^{-1}
are their corresponding right branch inverses,
then they are concave, strictly increasing continuous functions defined on the domain
:

G_{+}^{-1}\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty),\quad\ \psi_{+}^{-1}\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty),\quad\ H_{+}^{-1}\vcentcolon[-e^{-1},\infty)\to[e^{-1},\infty).

Moreover,
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})(y,z)
given in (
6.2
) are well defined for
(y,z)\in(0,\infty)^{2}

such that, for each fixed
z>0
,
y\mapsto f_{i}(y;z)
is positive monotone increasing
for
i=1,2,3
, and

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}f_{i}(y;z)=0\qquad\,\,\text{for $i=1,2,3$}.

Proof.

First,
G(\zeta)
and
\psi(\zeta)
are uniformly convex and achieve their minimum
at
\zeta=1
with
G(1)=0
.
Hence,
G(\zeta)
and
\psi(\zeta)
are strictly increasing in
[1,\infty)
.
Thus, the inverse functions
\psi_{+}^{-1},G_{+}^{-1}\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)

exist and satisfy
G(G_{+}^{-1}(\xi))=\psi(\psi_{+}^{-1}(\xi))=\xi

for all
\xi\in[0,\infty)
.
In addition,
H(\zeta)
is uniformly convex and achieves its minimum
at
\zeta=e^{-1}
with
H(e^{-1})=-e^{-1}
.
Hence,
H(\zeta)
is strictly increasing in
[e^{-1},\infty)
.
Then the inverse function
H_{+}^{-1}\vcentcolon[e^{-1},\infty)\to[-e^{-1},\infty)

exists and satisfies
H(H_{+}^{-1}(\xi))=\xi
for all
\xi\in[-e^{-1},\infty)
.
Since
H(1)=0
, the restricted function
H_{+}^{-1}\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[1,\infty)

is monotone increasing with
H_{+}^{-1}(0)=1
.

Next, by the inverse function theorem,

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}G_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(\xi)=\Big(\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}G}{{\mathrm{d}}\zeta}\big(G_{+}^{-1}(\xi)\big)\Big)^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{\log\big(G_{+}^{-1}(\xi)\big)}\qquad\ \text{for $\xi\in(0,\infty)$}.

Since
\xi\mapsto G_{+}^{-1}(\xi)
is a monotone increasing bijective map
and
G_{+}^{-1}(\xi)\to\infty
as
\xi\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\xi\to\infty}\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}G_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(\xi)=\lim\limits_{\xi\to\infty}\dfrac{1}{\log\big(G_{+}^{-1}(\xi)\big)}=0,

which, along with the L’Hôpital rule, yields that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}f_{1}(y;z)\vcentcolon=\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}yG_{+}^{-1}(\dfrac{z}{y})\overset{\text{H}}{=}z\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}G_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(\dfrac{z}{y})=0.

In addition,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial y}(y;z)=G_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})-\dfrac{zy^{-1}}{\log\big(G_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})\big)}\qquad\ \text{for $(y,z)\in(0,\infty)^{2}$},

where
G_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})\in(1,\infty)
for each
(y,z)\in(0,\infty)^{2}
.
It follows from
G(\zeta)=1-\zeta+\zeta\log\zeta
and the identity
G_{+}^{-1}(G(\zeta))=\zeta

that

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial y}(y;z)=\dfrac{G_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})-1}{\log\big(G_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})\big)}>0\qquad\ \text{for all $(y,z)\in(0,\infty)^{2}$},

which implies that, for fixing
z>0
,
f_{1}(y;z)
is monotone increasing for
y\in(0,\infty)
.

Next, by the inverse function theorem, it follows that

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(\xi)=\Big(\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}\psi}{{\mathrm{d}}\zeta}\big(\psi_{+}^{-1}(\xi)\big)\Big)^{-1}=\Big(1-\dfrac{1}{\psi_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})}\Big)^{-1}\qquad\ \text{for $\xi\in(0,\infty)$}.

Since
\psi_{+}^{-1}(\xi)\to\infty
as
\xi\to\infty
, for each fixed
z>0
, by the L’Hôpital rule,
we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}y\psi_{+}^{-1}(\dfrac{z}{y})\overset{\text{H}}{=}z\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}\Big(1-\dfrac{1}{\psi_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})}\Big)^{-1}=z\quad\ \text{for $z>0$}.

Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}f_{2}(y;z)=-z+\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}y\psi_{+}^{-1}(\dfrac{z}{y})=0.

Moreover, fixing
z>0
and taking the derivative of
y\mapsto f_{2}(y;z)
, one has

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial f_{2}}{\partial y}(y;z)=\psi_{+}^{-1}(\dfrac{z}{y})-\dfrac{z}{y^{2}}\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}\psi_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(\dfrac{z}{y})=\dfrac{\psi_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})\log(\psi_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1}))}{\psi_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})-1}>0\quad\text{ for $y\in(0,\infty)$},

which implies that
y\to f_{2}(y;z)
is increasing for each fixed
z>0
.

Finally, by the inverse function theorem, it follows that

\displaystyle\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}H_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(\xi)=\Big(\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}H}{{\mathrm{d}}\zeta}(H_{+}^{-1}(\xi))\Big)^{-1}=\dfrac{1}{1+\log(H_{+}^{-1}(\xi))}\qquad\ \text{for $\xi\in[0,\infty)$}.

Since
H_{+}^{-1}(\xi)\to\infty
as
\xi\to\infty
,

\lim\limits_{\xi\to\infty}\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}H_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(\xi)=\lim\limits_{\xi\to\infty}\dfrac{1}{1+\log(H_{+}^{-1}(\xi))}=0,

which, along with the L’Hôpital rule, implies that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}f_{3}(y;z)\overset{\text{H}}{=}\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}\dfrac{-zy^{-2}}{-y^{-2}}\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}H_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(zy^{-1})=\lim\limits_{y\searrow 0}\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}H_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(zy^{-1})=0.

Furthermore, for fixing
z>0
, we see that, for all
y\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial f_{3}}{\partial y}(y;z)=H_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})-\dfrac{z}{y}\dfrac{{\mathrm{d}}H_{+}^{-1}}{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}(zy^{-1})=\dfrac{H_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})}{1+\log H_{+}^{-1}(zy^{-1})}>0.

Therefore,
y\mapsto f_{3}(y;z)
is strictly increasing and positive for each fixed
z>0
.
∎

Before proving the next proposition, we state the so-called Mazur’s Lemma:

Theorem B.1

(Mazur’s Lemma)
.

Let
(B,\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{B})
be a Banach space, and let
S\subseteq B
be a convex subset.
Then
S
is closed in the strong topology if and only if it is closed in the weak topology.

For further detail, we refer the reader to Theorem 3.7 in Chapter 3.3 in
[
3
]
.

Proposition B.2
.

Let
d>0
,
T>0
, and
G(\zeta)\vcentcolon=1-\zeta+\zeta\log\zeta
.
Assume there exist constants
\widetilde{C}_{1},\widetilde{C}_{2}>0
,
\widetilde{\delta}\geq 0
,
and a sequence
\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}([d,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))

satisfying
:

\begin{cases}\theta_{i}-1\rightharpoonup\theta-1\quad\text{ weakly in $L^{2}\big(0,T;L^{2}([d,\infty),r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)\big)$},\\
\sup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{\infty}G(\theta_{i})(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\widetilde{C}_{1},\\
\widetilde{\delta}\leq\theta_{i}\leq\widetilde{C}_{2}\quad\,\,\text{for {\it a.e.} $(r,t)\in[d,\infty)\times[0,T]$ and for each $i\in\mathbb{N}$}.\end{cases}

(B.1)

Then the limit function
\theta
satisfies

\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{\infty}G(\theta)(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\widetilde{C}_{1}.

Proof.

Fix an integer
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Denote
B_{N}\vcentcolon=L^{2}\big(0,T;L^{2}([d,N],r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)\big)
.
Then
B_{N}
is a Banach space.
Set
S_{N}
to be the subset:

\displaystyle S_{N}\vcentcolon=\Big\{w\in B_{N}\vcentcolon\ \esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{N}G(w)(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\widetilde{C}_{1},\,\,\widetilde{\delta}\leq w(r,t)\leq\widetilde{C}_{2}\ \text{{\it a.e.} $(r,t)\in[d,N]\times[0,T]$}\Big\}.

First, we claim that
S_{N}
is a convex subset of
B_{N}
.
Since
G(z)=1-z+z\log z
is convex, it follows that, for each
w_{1},w_{2}\in S_{N}
and
0<\eta<1
,

\displaystyle\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{N}G(\eta w_{1}+(1-\eta)w_{2})(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle\quad\leq\eta\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{N}G(w_{1})(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r+(1-\eta)\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{N}G(w_{2})(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\widetilde{C}_{1},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}\leq\big(\eta w_{1}+(1-\eta)w_{2}\big)(r,t)\leq\widetilde{C}_{2}\qquad\ \text{for {\it a.e.} $(r,t)\in[d,N]\times[0,T]$},

which implies that
S_{N}
is indeed a convex subset of
B_{N}
.

We next show that, if
\{w_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset S_{N}
is a sequence such that,
for some
w\in B_{N}
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\lVert w_{i}-w\rVert_{B_{N}}=\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{d}^{N}\lvert w_{i}-w\rvert^{2}(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t=0,

then
w\in S_{N}
.

Since
w_{i}\to w
strongly in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}([d,N],r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
,
there exists a subsequence (still denoted)
\{w_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

w_{i}(r,t)\to w(r,t)\qquad\text{for {\it a.e.} $(r,t)\in[d,N]\times[0,T]$}.

(B.2)

Since
\{w_{i}\}_{i}\subset S_{N}
, the convergence in (
B.2
) implies that

\widetilde{\delta}\leq w(r,t)\leq\widetilde{C}_{2}\qquad\text{for {\it a.e.} $(r,t)\in[d,N]\times[0,T]$}.

(B.3)

Since
G(\cdot)
is convex and achieves its minimum at
G(\zeta=1)=0
,
and
G(\zeta=0)=1
,
it follows that, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
with
\widetilde{\delta}\leq w_{i}\leq\widetilde{C}_{2}
,

G(w_{i}(r,t))\leq\max\{1,G(\widetilde{\delta}),\,G(\widetilde{C}_{2})\}
.
Moreover, since
G(\cdot)
is continuous, it follows from (
B.2
) that

G(w_{i}(r,t))\to G(w(r,t))
as
i\to\infty
for
a.e.

(r,t)\in[d,N]\times[0,T]
.
By the dominated convergence theorem, we see that, for each
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T
,

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{t_{2}-t_{1}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{d}^{N}G(w)(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t=\lim\limits_{i\to\infty}\dfrac{1}{t_{2}-t_{1}}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{d}^{N}G(w_{i})(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r{\mathrm{d}}t\leq\widetilde{C}_{1}.

Since this is true for any interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]\subseteq[0,T]
,
then, by the Lebesgue differentiation theorem,

\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{N}G(w)(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\widetilde{C}_{1}.

(B.4)

It follows from (
B.3
)–(
B.4
)
that
w\in S_{N}
, which, along with Theorem
B.1
, implies that

S_{N}\ \text{ is closed in the weak topology of $B_{N}\vcentcolon=L^{2}\big(0,T;L^{2}([d,N],r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r)\big)$}.

(B.5)

By
(
B.2
)
, it follows that
\{\theta_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset S_{N}

and
\theta_{i}\rightharpoonup\theta
in
B_{N}=L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}([d,N],r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r))
.
Assertion (
B.5
) then implies that
\theta\in S_{N}
and, in particular,

\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{N}G(\theta)(r,t)\,r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\widetilde{C}_{1}.

Now, since this is true for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and
G(\cdot)
is non-negative,
it follows from the monotone convergence theorem that

\displaystyle\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{\infty}G(\theta)(r,t)r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\esssup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{d}^{N}G(\theta)(r,t)r^{m}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\widetilde{C}_{1}.

∎
